our next theorem precisely characterizes sa and ra in the separable regime and generalizes theorem 4.3 to the anisotropic case. before proceeding to state the theorem we need to establish some definitions and assumptions.

definition 6.2 for a given matrix  $a \geq 0$  and a function  $f$ , we define the weighted moreau envelope of  $f$  as follows:

$$
e _ {f, \boldsymbol {a}} (\boldsymbol {x}; \lambda) := \min _ {\boldsymbol {v}} \frac {1}{2} \| \boldsymbol {x} - \boldsymbol {v} \| _ {\boldsymbol {a}} ^ {2} + \lambda f (\boldsymbol {v})
$$

when  $\mathbf{a} = \mathbf{i}$ , we recover the (scaled) classical moreau envelope. we denote by  $e_{q,\sigma}$  the weighted moreau envelope corresponding to  $\| \cdot \|_{\ell_q}^q$  function.

assumption 6 for the sequence of instances  $\{\pmb {\sigma}(n),\pmb {\mu}(n),d(n)\}_{n\in \mathbb{n}}$  indexed by  $n$ , we assume that:

(a) the following (in probability) limit exists for any scalars  $c_{0}, c_{1}, \lambda_{0}, \eta \in \mathbb{r}_{+}$ :

$$
\mathsf {f} \left(c _ {0}, c _ {1}; b _ {0}, b _ {1}\right) := \lim  _ {n \rightarrow \infty} e _ {q, \boldsymbol {i} + b _ {0} \boldsymbol {\sigma}} \left(\left(\boldsymbol {i} + b _ {0} \boldsymbol {\sigma}\right) ^ {- 1} \left\{\frac {c _ {0}}{2 \sqrt {n}} \boldsymbol {\sigma} ^ {1 / 2} \mathsf {p} _ {\boldsymbol {\mu}} ^ {\perp} \boldsymbol {h} - \frac {c _ {1}}{2} \widetilde {\boldsymbol {\mu}} \right\}; b _ {1} \| \boldsymbol {\mu} \| _ {\ell_ {p}} ^ {q}\right).
$$

(b) the empirical distribution of eigenvalues of  $\pmb{\sigma}$  converges weakly to a distribution  $\rho$  with stieltjes transform  $s_{\rho}(z)\coloneqq \int \frac{\rho(t)}{z - t}\mathrm{d}t.$

with these definitions and assumptions in place we are ready to state our result in the separable regime.

theorem 6.3 consider a data set generated i.i.d. according to an anisotropic gaussian mixture data model per section 2.2 with a spiked covariance per assumption 5. also suppose the mean vector  $\pmb{\mu}$  and covariance matrix  $\pmb{\sigma}$  obey assumptions 2, 3, and 6. also let  $\widetilde{\pmb{\theta}}^{\varepsilon}$  be the max margin solution per (f.2). if  $\delta < \delta_{*}$ , with  $\delta_{*}$  given by (4.3), then in the asymptotic setting of assumption 1 we have:

(a) the following convex-concave minimax scalar optimization problem has bounded solution  $(\alpha_{*},\gamma_{0*},\theta_{*},\beta_{*},\lambda_{0*},\eta_{*},\tilde{\eta}_{*})$  with the minimization components  $(\alpha_{*},\gamma_{0*},\theta_{*})$  unique:

$$
\begin{array}{l} \min  _ {\alpha , \gamma_ {0} \geq 0, \theta   \beta , \lambda_ {0}, \eta \geq 0, \tilde {\eta}} \max  _ {\beta , \lambda_ {0}, \eta \tilde {\eta}} d _ {\mathrm {s}} (\alpha , \gamma_ {0}, \theta , \beta , \lambda_ {0}, \eta , \tilde {\eta}), \quad w h e r e \\ d _ {\mathrm {s}} (\alpha , \gamma_ {0}, \theta , \beta , \lambda_ {0}, \eta , \tilde {\eta}) = 2 \mathsf {f} \left(\beta , \tilde {\eta}; \frac {\eta}{2 \alpha}, \frac {\lambda_ {0}}{q \gamma_ {0} ^ {q - 1}}\right) - \frac {\beta^ {2} \alpha}{2 \delta \eta} \left(1 + \frac {2 \alpha}{\eta} s _ {\rho} \left(- \frac {2 \alpha}{\eta}\right)\right) \\ - \frac {2 \lambda_ {0}}{q} \gamma_ {0} - \frac {\eta \alpha}{2} - \tilde {\eta} \theta \\ - \frac {\tilde {\eta} ^ {2}}{4 \left(1 + \frac {\eta}{2 \alpha} a ^ {2}\right)} + \beta \sqrt {\mathbb {e} \left[ \left(\left(1 + \varepsilon_ {0} \gamma_ {0} - \theta v\right) + \alpha g\right) _ {+} ^ {2} \right]}, \tag {6.2} \\ \end{array}
$$

with expectation in last part taken with respect to  $g\sim \mathsf{n}(0,1)$

(b) it holds in probability that

$$
\lim  _ {n \rightarrow \infty} \frac {1}{\| \boldsymbol {\mu} \| _ {\ell_ {2}}} \left\langle \boldsymbol {\mu}, \widetilde {\boldsymbol {\theta}} ^ {\varepsilon} \right\rangle = \theta_ {*}, \tag {6.3}
$$

$$
\lim  _ {n \rightarrow \infty} \left\| \widetilde {\boldsymbol {\theta}} ^ {\varepsilon} \right\| _ {\ell_ {2}} = \alpha_ {*} , \tag {6.4}
$$

$$
\lim  _ {n \rightarrow \infty} \| \boldsymbol {\mu} \| _ {\ell_ {p}} \| \widetilde {\boldsymbol {\theta}} ^ {\varepsilon} \| _ {\ell_ {q}} = \gamma_ {0 *}. \tag {6.5}
$$

(c) as a corollary of part (b) and lemma 2.1, the following limits hold in probability:

$$
\lim  _ {n \rightarrow \infty} \mathsf {s a} \left(\widetilde {\boldsymbol {\theta}} ^ {\varepsilon}\right) = \phi \left(v \frac {\theta_ {*}}{\alpha_ {*}}\right), \tag {6.6}
$$

$$
\lim  _ {n \rightarrow \infty} \mathsf {r a} \left(\widetilde {\boldsymbol {\theta}} ^ {\varepsilon}\right) = \phi \left(- \frac {\varepsilon_ {0} \gamma_ {0 *}}{\alpha_ {*}} + v \frac {\theta_ {*}}{\alpha_ {*}}\right). \tag {6.7}
$$

next we turn our attention to characterizing sa and ra on the non-separable regime. to state result we need an additional assumption

assumption 7 for the sequence of instances  $\{\pmb {\sigma}(n),\pmb {\mu}(n),p(n)\}_{n\in \mathbb{n}}$  indexed by  $n$ , we assume that the following (in probability) limit exists for any scalars  $c_{0},c_{1}\in \mathbb{r}_{+}$  and  $\lambda_0\in \mathbb{r}$ :

$$
\mathsf {e} \left(c _ {0}, c _ {1}; \lambda_ {0}\right) := \lim  _ {n \rightarrow \infty} e _ {q, \boldsymbol {\sigma}} \left(\frac {c _ {0}}{\sqrt {n}} \boldsymbol {\sigma} ^ {- 1 / 2} \boldsymbol {h} - c _ {1} \widetilde {\boldsymbol {\mu}}; \lambda_ {0} \| \boldsymbol {\mu} \| _ {\ell_ {p}} ^ {q}\right), \tag {6.8}
$$

where we recall  $\widetilde{\pmb{\mu}} = \pmb {\mu} / \| \pmb {\mu}\|_{\ell_2}$

our next theorem generalizes theorem 4.5 to anisotropic case.

theorem 6.4 consider a data set generated i.i.d. according to an anisotropic gaussian mixture data model per section 2.2 with a spiked covariance per assumption 5. also suppose the mean vector  $\pmb{\mu}$  and covariance matrix  $\pmb{\sigma}$  obey assumptions 2, 3, and 7. also let  $\widehat{\pmb{\theta}}^{\varepsilon}$  be the solution to optimization (2.3). if  $\delta >\delta_{*}$ , with  $\delta_{*}$  given by (4.3), then in the asymptotic setting of assumption 1 we have:

(a) the following convex-concave minimax scalar optimization problem has bounded solution  $(\theta_{*},\alpha_{*},\gamma_{0*},\tau_{g*},\beta_{*},\tau_{h*})$  with the minimization components  $(\alpha_{*},\gamma_{0*},\theta_{*})$  unique:

$$
\min  _ {\theta , 0 \leq \alpha , \gamma_ {0}, \tau_ {g}} \max  _ {0 \leq \beta , \tau_ {h}} d _ {\mathrm {n s}} (\alpha , \gamma_ {0}, \theta , \tau_ {g}, \beta , \tau_ {h})
$$

$$
\begin{array}{l} d _ {\mathrm {n s}} (\alpha , \gamma_ {0}, \theta , \tau_ {g}, \beta , \tau_ {h}) = \frac {\beta \tau_ {g}}{2} + l \left(\sqrt {\alpha^ {2} + a ^ {2} \theta^ {2}}, v \theta - \varepsilon_ {0} \gamma_ {0}, \frac {\tau_ {g}}{\beta}\right) \\ - \min _ {\lambda_ {0} \geq 0, \nu} \left[ \frac {\alpha}{\tau_ {h}} \left\{\frac {\beta^ {2}}{2 \delta} + \lambda_ {0} \left(\frac {\gamma_ {0} \tau_ {h}}{\alpha}\right) ^ {q} + \frac {\nu^ {2}}{2} - \mathsf {e} \left(\beta , \left(\frac {\tau_ {h} \theta}{\alpha} + \frac {\nu}{a}\right); \lambda_ {0}\right) \right\} + \frac {\alpha \tau_ {h}}{2} \right]. \\ \end{array}
$$

(b) it holds in probability that

$$
\lim  _ {n \rightarrow \infty} \frac {1}{\| \boldsymbol {\mu} \| _ {\ell_ {2}}} \left\langle \boldsymbol {\mu}, \widehat {\boldsymbol {\theta}} ^ {\varepsilon} \right\rangle = \theta_ {*}, \tag {6.10}
$$

$$
\lim  _ {n \rightarrow \infty} \| p _ {\mu} ^ {\perp} \widehat {\boldsymbol {\theta}} ^ {\varepsilon} \| _ {\ell_ {2}} = \alpha_ {*}, \tag {6.11}
$$

$$
\lim  _ {n \rightarrow \infty} \| \boldsymbol {\mu} \| _ {\ell_ {p}} \| \widehat {\boldsymbol {\theta}} ^ {\varepsilon} \| _ {\ell_ {q}} = \gamma_ {0 *}. \tag {6.12}
$$

(c) as a corollary of part (b) and lemma 2.1, the following limits hold in probability:

$$
\lim  _ {n \rightarrow \infty} \mathsf {s a} \left(\widehat {\boldsymbol {\theta}} ^ {\widetilde {\varepsilon}}\right) = \phi \left(\frac {v \theta_ {*}}{\sqrt {\alpha_ {*} ^ {2} + a ^ {2} \theta_ {*} ^ {2}}}\right), \tag {6.13}
$$

$$
\lim  _ {n \rightarrow \infty} \mathrm {r a} \left(\widehat {\boldsymbol {\theta}} ^ {\varepsilon}\right) = \phi \left(\frac {- \varepsilon_ {0} \gamma_ {0 *} + v \theta_ {*}}{\sqrt {\alpha_ {*} ^ {2} + a ^ {2} \theta_ {*} ^ {2}}}\right). \tag {6.14}
$$

the results above generalize out results to the anisotropic case. the reader may of course be wondering when assumptions 6 and 7 hold. this is the subject of the next remark which we prove in appendix b.4.

remark 6.1 for the case of  $\ell_2$  perturbation ( $p = q = 2$ ), the following two conditions are sufficient for assumption 6 and 7 to hold:

(i) the empirical distribution of the entries of  $\sqrt{d}\pmb{\mu}$  converges weakly to a distribution  $\mathbb{p}_m$  on real line, with bounded second moment, i.e.  $\int x^{2}\mathrm{d}\mathbb{p}_{m}(x) = \sigma_{m,2}^{2} < \infty$ .  
(ii) the empirical distribution of eigenvalues of  $\pmb{\sigma}$  converges weakly to a distribution  $\rho$  with stieltjes transform  $s_{\rho}(z)\coloneqq \int \frac{\rho(t)}{z - t}\mathrm{d}t.$

# 7 proof sketch and mathematical challenges

our theoretical results on adversarial training for binary classification fits in the rapidly growing recent literature on developing sharp high-dimensional asymptotics of (possibly non-smooth) convex optimization-based estimators [dmm11, sto09, bm12, almt13, sto13, djm13, oth13, toh15, kar13, ek18, dm16, ors17, mm18, wwm19, cm19, hl19, bkrs19]. most of this line of work focuses on linear models and regression problems. it has been only recently that the literature witnessed a surge of interest in sharp analysis of a variety of methods tailored to binary classification models [hua17, cs20, sc19, mlc19, ka19, sah19, tpt20, dkt19, mrsy19, ls20, mklz20, tpt20, lol20]. however, none of these papers study adversarial training and its impact on standard/robust accuracies.

on a technical level, our sharp analysis relies on the convex gaussian min-max theorem (cgmt) [toh15] (see also [sto13, oth13, ors17]), which is a powerful extension of the gordon's gaussian comparison inequality [gor88]. we refer to section 7 for an overview of this framework and the mathematical challenges we encounter in applying it to our adversarial setting. we next present a proof sketch for deriving our main results which illustrates the key ideas.

to be able to provide a precise characterization of the various tradeoffs we need to develop a precise understanding of the adversarial training objective

$$
\min  _ {\boldsymbol {\theta} \in \mathbb {r} ^ {d}} \mathcal {l} (\boldsymbol {\theta}) := \frac {1}{n} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \ell \left(y _ {i} \langle \boldsymbol {x} _ {i}, \boldsymbol {\theta} \rangle - \varepsilon \| \boldsymbol {\theta} \| _ {\ell_ {q}}\right), \tag {7.1}
$$

and its optimal solution  $\widehat{\pmb{\theta}}^{\varepsilon} \in \arg \min_{\pmb{\theta} \in \mathbb{r}^{d}} \mathcal{l}(\pmb{\theta})$ . given the classification nature of the problem, as discussed earlier, we have to study this loss in the two different regimes of separable and non-separable as well as characterize the threshold of separability. in this section we wish to provide a brief overview of the steps of our proofs and some of the challenges. we focus our exposition on the non-separable case. while the details of the derivations for the separable case and the calculation of the separability threshold differ from the non-separable case the general steps are similar and therefore the steps below also provides a general road map for the proof of these results as well. specifically, our proofs in the non-separable regime consists of the following steps:

# step i: reformulation of the loss.

the loss (7.1), while significantly simplified due to the removal of the max function, is still rather complicated and precisely characterizing the behavior and the quality of its optimal solution is still challenging. in particular, the dependence on the random data matrix  $\mathbf{x}$  is still rather complex hindering statistical analysis even in an asymptotic setting. to bring the optimization problem into a form more amenable to precise asymptotic analysis we carry out a series of reformulations of the

optimization problem. combining these reformulation steps we arrive at the following equivalent primal optimization (po) problem

$$
\min _ {\boldsymbol {\theta}, \boldsymbol {v} \in \mathbb {r} ^ {n}} \max _ {\boldsymbol {u} \in \mathbb {r} ^ {n}} \frac {1}{n} \left\{\boldsymbol {u} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {1} \boldsymbol {\mu} ^ {t} \boldsymbol {\theta} + \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {d _ {y}} \boldsymbol {z} \boldsymbol {\sigma} ^ {1 / 2} \boldsymbol {\theta} - \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {v} \right\} + \frac {1}{n} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \ell \left(v _ {i} - \varepsilon \| \boldsymbol {\theta} \| _ {\ell_ {q}}\right) \tag {7.2}
$$

# step ii: reduction to an auxiliary optimization (ao) problem.

the equivalent form above may be counter-intuitive as we started by simplifying a different mini-max optimization problem and we have now again introduced a new maximization! the main advantage of this new form is that it is in fact affine in the data matrix  $\mathbf{x}$ . this particular form allows us to use a powerful extension of a classical gaussian process inequality due to [gor88] known as convex gaussian minimax theorem (cgmt) [toh15] which focuses on characterizing the asymptotic behavior of mini-max optimization problems that are affine in a gaussian matrix  $\mathbf{x}$ . this result enables us to characterize the properties of (7.1) by studying the asymptotic behavior of the following, arguable simpler, auxiliary optimization (ao) problem instead

$$
\begin{array}{l} \min  _ {\boldsymbol {\theta}, v \in \mathbb {r} ^ {n}} \max  _ {\boldsymbol {u} \in \mathbb {r} ^ {n}} \frac {1}{n} \left\{\left\| \mathrm {p} _ {\mu} ^ {\perp} \boldsymbol {\sigma} ^ {1 / 2} \boldsymbol {\theta} \right\| _ {\ell_ {2}} \boldsymbol {g} ^ {t} \boldsymbol {d} _ {\boldsymbol {y}} \boldsymbol {u} + \left\| \boldsymbol {d} _ {\boldsymbol {y}} \boldsymbol {u} \right\| _ {\ell_ {2}} \boldsymbol {h} ^ {t} \mathrm {p} _ {\mu} ^ {\perp} \boldsymbol {\sigma} ^ {1 / 2} \boldsymbol {\theta} \right. \\ \left. + \left(\boldsymbol {u} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {d _ {y}} \boldsymbol {z}\right) \left(\widetilde {\boldsymbol {\mu}} ^ {t} \boldsymbol {\sigma} ^ {1 / 2} \boldsymbol {\theta}\right) + \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\top} \mathbf {1} \boldsymbol {\mu} ^ {t} \boldsymbol {\theta} - \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {v} \right\} + \frac {1}{n} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \ell \left(v _ {i} - \varepsilon \left\| \boldsymbol {\theta} \right\| _ {\ell_ {q}}\right), \tag {7.3} \\ \end{array}
$$

where  $\pmb{g} \sim \mathsf{n}(0, \pmb{i}_n)$  and  $\pmb{h} \sim \mathsf{n}(0, \pmb{i}_d)$ ,  $\mathsf{p}_{\pmb{\mu}}^{\perp} := \pmb{i} - \widetilde{\pmb{\mu}} \widetilde{\pmb{\mu}}^t$ , and  $\mathsf{p}_{\pmb{\mu}} := \widetilde{\pmb{\mu}} \widetilde{\pmb{\mu}}^t$ .

we emphasize that the relationship between the above po problem (7.2) and how it is exactly related to the ao problem (7.3) is more intricate and technical compared with classical cgmt and related work in the context of classification [sah19, tpt20]. in particular, prior work on binary classification such as [sah19, tpt20] via cgmt (which corresponds to the non-robust case i.e.  $\varepsilon = 0$ ) utilize the fact that (7.2) is rotationally invariant and hence one can assume  $\pmb{\mu} = \pmb{e}_1$  without loss of generality. however, in the robust version (unless  $p = q = 2$ ) the direction of  $\pmb{\mu}$  plays a crucial role due to the regularization term  $\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\ell\left(v_i - \varepsilon\|\pmb{\theta}\|_{\ell_q}\right)$ .

# step iii: scalarization of the auxiliary optimization (ao) problem.

in this step we further simplify the ao problem in (7.3). in particular we show the asymptotic behavior of the ao can be characterized rather precisely via the scalar optimization problem

$$
\begin{array}{l} \min  _ {\theta , 0 \leq \alpha , \gamma_ {0}, \tau_ {g}} \max  _ {0 \leq \beta , \tau_ {h}} d _ {\mathrm {n s}} (\alpha , \gamma_ {0}, \theta , \tau_ {g}, \beta , \tau_ {h}) := \frac {\beta \tau_ {g}}{2} + l \left(\sqrt {\alpha^ {2} + a ^ {2} \theta^ {2}}, v \theta - \varepsilon_ {0} \gamma_ {0}, \frac {\tau_ {g}}{\beta}\right) \\ - \min  _ {\lambda_ {0} \geq 0, \nu} \left[ \frac {\alpha}{\tau_ {h}} \left\{\frac {\beta^ {2}}{2 \delta} + \lambda_ {0} \left(\frac {\gamma_ {0} \tau_ {h}}{\alpha}\right) ^ {q} + \frac {\nu^ {2}}{2} - e \left(\beta , \left(\frac {\tau_ {h} \theta}{\alpha} + \frac {\nu}{a}\right); \lambda_ {0}\right) \right\} + \frac {\alpha \tau_ {h}}{2} \right]. \tag {7.4} \\ \end{array}
$$

more specifically, a variety of conclusions can be derived based on the optimal solutions of the above optimization problem as we discuss in the next step. we note that while this expression may look complicated we prove that this optimization problem is in fact convex in the minimization parameters  $(\theta, \alpha, \tau_g)$  and concave in the maximization parameters  $(\beta, \tau_h)$  so that its optimal solutions can be easily derived via a simple low-dimensional gradient descent rather quickly and accurately. we also note that this proof is quite intricate and involved, so it is not possible to give an intuitive sketch of the arguments here. we refer to section b for details. however, we briefly state a few mathematical challenges that is unique to simplifying (7.3). first, the ao (7.3) does not have a

simple regularization whose scalarization reduces to a simple mean width calculation as in most simple cgmt uses. instead the regularization has a complicated form  $\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\ell\left(v_i - \varepsilon\|\pmb{\theta}\|_{\ell_q}\right)$  which requires rather intricate and involved scalarization calculations. second, this ao regularization term is not separable in  $\pmb{\theta}$  which significantly complicates the scalarization of the ao. finally, we handle the case of more general covariance matrices where  $\pmb{\sigma} \neq \pmb{i}$ .

# step iv: completing the proof of the theorems.

finally, we utilize the above scalar form to derive all of the different theorems and results. this is done by relating the quantities of interest in each theorem to the optimal solutions of (7.4). for instance, we show that  $\lim_{n\to \infty}\mathsf{sa}(\widehat{\pmb{\theta}}^{\varepsilon}) = \phi \left(\frac{v\theta_{*}}{\sqrt{\alpha_{*}^{2} + a^{2}\theta_{*}^{2}}}\right)$  and  $\lim_{n\to \infty}\mathsf{ra}(\widehat{\pmb{\theta}}^{\varepsilon}) = \phi \left(\frac{-\varepsilon_0\gamma_{0*} + v\theta_*}{\sqrt{\alpha_*^2 + a^2\theta_*^2}}\right)$  where  $\alpha_{*}$  and  $\theta_{*}$  are the optimal solutions over  $\alpha$  and  $\theta$ . these calculations/proofs are carried out in detail in section b. since each argument is different we do not provide a summary here and refer to the corresponding sections.

# 8 discussion

we conclude the paper by discussing some of the potential extensions and applications of our theory as well as comparison with more classical approaches to binary classification.

# 8.1 generalization to random features models

while our focus in this paper was on linear classifiers, these models are quite foundational and serve as the basis for more complex models. for instance, one potential generalization of our results is to the class of random features models given by

$$
\mathcal {f} _ {\mathrm {r f}} := \left\{f (\boldsymbol {x}; \boldsymbol {\theta}, \boldsymbol {w}) = \operatorname {s i g n} \left(\left\langle \boldsymbol {\theta}, \sigma (\boldsymbol {w} \boldsymbol {x}) \right\rangle\right): \quad \boldsymbol {\theta} \in \mathbb {r} ^ {n} \right\},
$$

where  $\pmb{x} \in \mathbb{r}^d$  represents the feature vector,  $\pmb{w} \in \mathbb{r}^{n \times d}$  is a random matrix whose rows are chosen uniformly at random from the unit sphere in  $d$ -dimension, and  $\sigma: \mathbb{r} \mapsto \mathbb{r}$  is a nonlinear function (for a vector  $\pmb{v}$ ,  $\sigma(\pmb{v}) = (\sigma(v_1), \dots, \sigma(v_m))$  is applied entry-wise). random features model can also be described as a two-layer fully connected neural network with random first-layer weights fixed to  $\pmb{w}$  and not optimized, while the second layer weights are represented by vector  $\pmb{\theta}$  and are optimized over to minimize the loss of interest. the random features model was introduced by [rr07] for scaling kernel methods to large datasets and there has been a large body of work drawing connections between random features models, kernel methods and fully trained neural networks [dfs16, dan17, jgh18, ll18].

an intriguing phenomenon, pointed out by [mm19, mrsy19] from the analysis of random features model in non-adversarial contexts, is that the random features model has the same asymptotic behavior as a simpler noisy linear features model whose second order statistics match the nonlinear random features model, namely a linear model with noisy features  $\pmb{u} \in \mathbb{r}^{n}$  given by  $\pmb{u} = \eta_{0} + \eta_{1}\pmb{w}\pmb{x} + \eta_{2}\pmb{z}$ , where  $\pmb{z}$  has i.i.d standard normal entries, independent of  $\pmb{w}$  and  $\pmb{x}$ . also, the constants  $\eta_{0},\eta_{1},\eta_{2}$  depend on the activation function  $\sigma(\cdot)$  and are chosen so that the two models have the same first and second moments. a promising direction is to establish a similar connection for an adversarial setting and use our theory (relied on cgmt framework) to analyze the equivalent noisy linear model, from which we obtain an asymptotic characterization for adversarial training under the random features model. very recently and after this paper was posted, [hj22] has pursued a similar approach to

![](images/fac4c656e07ebce7a72fb0dafd17fa89767a7d78b3e2466832f825fbb3703923.jpg)  
figure 8: robust accuracy curves versus  $\varepsilon_0$  for different choices of  $\delta$ , and the perturbation norm  $\ell_1$  ( $p = 1$ ). the optimal choice of  $\varepsilon_0$  for the robust minimax estimator decreases with  $\delta$ .

precisely characterize the role of overparametrization on robust generalization of random features in a regression setting.

# 8.2 optimal  $\varepsilon_0$  for the robust minimax estimator

an interesting application of our theory is to derive the optimal value  $\varepsilon_0^{\mathrm{op}}$  (perceived perturbation level) in the robust minimax estimator (2.2), while fixing the adversary's (actual) perturbation level on test inputs to  $\varepsilon_{0,\mathrm{test}}$ . (see remark 4.4 on how our theory applies to this setting.) the optimality here is with respect to maximizing the robust accuracy. somewhat surprisingly  $\varepsilon_0^{\mathrm{op}}$  is different than  $\varepsilon_{0,\mathrm{test}}$  in general and depends on  $\delta$  and the choice of perturbation norm  $\ell_p$  in a non-trivial way (there is no one-fit-all solution and this highlights the importance of having a precise theory to understand the effect of adversarial training which is the primary goal of the current work). for example, in the particular case of  $\varepsilon_{0,\mathrm{test}} = 0$ , the question reduces to finding the value of  $\varepsilon_0$  which maximizes standard accuracy. as we already discussed, the answer very much depends on  $\delta$  and  $p$ . for  $p = 2$ , we observe that (cf. figure 3(a)) adversarial training helps with improving the standard accuracy. however for  $p = \infty$ ,  $\varepsilon_0^{\mathrm{op}}$  should be large enough so that the problem becomes non-separable and also its value decreases as  $\delta$  increases (cf. figure 5(a)). as another example, we consider the case of  $\varepsilon_{0,\mathrm{test}} = 0.3$  with  $\ell_{\infty}$  perturbations. in figure 8 we plot the robust accuracy versus  $\varepsilon_0$ , and the dashed vertical lines show the value of  $\varepsilon_0^{\mathrm{op}}$ . as we see its value decreases by increasing  $\delta$ , however, its exact value requires a precise analysis.

# 8.3 comparison with linear discriminant analysis (lda)

a classical approach to binary classification under the gaussian-mixture model is the linear discriminant analysis. in comparing the robustness property of lda and the robust minimax estimator studied in this paper, we cannot say one estimator always outperforms the others. to further discuss this point, we consider the gaussian-mixture model with identity covariance  $\pmb{\sigma} = \pmb{i}$  and balanced classes. in this case, the lda estimator reduces to  $\widehat{\pmb{\mu}}^{\mathrm{lda}} = \frac{1}{n}\sum_{i = 1}^{n}y_{i}\pmb{x}_{i}$  and the corresponding classification rule given by  $\hat{y} = \mathrm{sign}(\langle \pmb{x},\widehat{\pmb{\mu}}^{\mathrm{lda}}\rangle)$ . in the supplementary [js20] (section a), we compare the robust accuracy of lda estimator with that of the robust minimax estimator  $\widehat{\pmb{\theta}}^{\varepsilon}$  for some choices of  $p$ . as we will discuss, the depending on  $p$  and the adversary's power  $\varepsilon_0$ , one

can outperform the other.

# a comparison with linear discriminant analysis (lda)

a classical approach to binary classification under the gaussian-mixture model is the linear discriminant analysis. in comparing the robustness property of lda and the robust minimax estimator studied in this paper, we cannot say one estimator always outperforms the others. to further discuss this point, we consider the gaussian-mixture model with identity covariance  $\pmb{\sigma} = \pmb{i}$  and balanced classes. in this case, the lda estimator reduces to  $\widehat{\pmb{\mu}}^{\mathrm{lda}} = \frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}y_{i}\pmb{x}_{i}$  and the corresponding classification rule given by  $\hat{y} = \mathrm{sign}(\langle \pmb{x},\widehat{\pmb{\mu}}^{\mathrm{lda}}\rangle)$ . under the gaussian-mixture model we have  $\pmb{x} = y\pmb{\mu} + \pmb{z}$  with  $\pmb{z} \sim \mathsf{n}(0,\pmb{i})$ . therefore,

$$
\widehat {\boldsymbol {\mu}} ^ {\mathrm {l d a}} = \frac {1}{n} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} y _ {i} (y _ {i} \boldsymbol {\mu} + \boldsymbol {z} _ {i}) = \boldsymbol {\mu} + \frac {1}{n} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} y _ {i} \boldsymbol {z} _ {i} = \boldsymbol {\mu} + \tilde {\boldsymbol {z}}, \quad \tilde {\boldsymbol {z}} \sim \mathsf {n} (\boldsymbol {0}, \frac {1}{n} \boldsymbol {i})
$$

for simplicity we assume that the class averages  $\pmb{\mu}$  is generated as  $\pmb{\mu} \sim (\mathbf{0}, \frac{1}{d}\pmb{i})$ , similar to the setting considered in the numerical experiments. in asymptotic regime of  $n \to \infty$  and  $n / d \to \delta$ , we have that in probability:

$$
\lim  _ {n \rightarrow \infty} \left\langle \boldsymbol {\mu}, \widehat {\boldsymbol {\mu}} ^ {\mathrm {l d a}} \right\rangle = \lim  _ {n \rightarrow \infty} \| \boldsymbol {\mu} \| _ {\ell_ {2}} ^ {2} = 1,
$$

$$
\begin{array}{l} \lim _ {n \to \infty} d ^ {1 / 2 - 1 / q} \left\| \widehat {\boldsymbol {\mu}} ^ {\mathrm {l d a}} \right\| _ {\ell_ {q}} = \lim _ {n \to \infty} d ^ {1 / 2 - 1 / q} \left\| \boldsymbol {\mu} + \tilde {\boldsymbol {z}} \right\| _ {\ell_ {q}} \\ = \lim _ {n \to \infty} d ^ {1 / 2 - 1 / q} \left(\frac {1}{d} + \frac {1}{n}\right) ^ {1 / 2} d ^ {1 / q} c _ {q} = \left(1 + \frac {1}{\delta}\right) ^ {1 / 2} c _ {q}, \\ \end{array}
$$

where in the first equation we used the fact that  $\langle \pmb{\mu},\tilde{\pmb{z}}\rangle \sim \mathsf{n}\big(0,\frac{1}{n}\| \pmb {\mu}\|_{\ell_2}^2\big)$  has vanishing variance as  $n\to \infty$  . in the second inequality,  $c_q$  is the  $q$  -th moment of standard normal distribution. recall that  $\varepsilon = \varepsilon_0\| \pmb {\mu}\|_{\ell_p}$  with  $1 / p + 1 / q = 1$  , and also  $\| \pmb {\mu}\|_{\ell_p}\rightarrow d^{1 / p - 1 / 2}c_p = d^{1 / 2 - 1 / q}c_p$  . using these identities along with the characterization of standard and robust accuracies given by lemma 2.1 of the paper, we arrive at

$$
\lim _ {n \to \infty} \mathsf {s a} (\widehat {\boldsymbol {\mu}} ^ {\mathrm {l d a}}) = \phi \left(\sqrt {\frac {\delta}{1 + \delta}}\right),
$$

$$
\lim _ {n \to \infty} \mathsf {r a} (\widehat {\pmb {\mu}} ^ {\mathrm {l d a}}) = \phi \left(\sqrt {\frac {\delta}{1 + \delta}} - \varepsilon_ {0} c _ {q} c _ {p}\right). \tag {a.1}
$$

we next compare the robust accuracy of lda estimator with that of the robust minimax estimator  $\widehat{\pmb{\theta}}^{\varepsilon}$  for some choices of  $p$ . as we will discuss, the depending on  $p$  and the adversary's power  $\varepsilon_0$ , one can outperform the other.

-  $(p = q = 2)$ . figure 9(a) compares  $\mathsf{ra}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{\mathrm{lda}})$  with  $\mathsf{ra}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{\varepsilon})$  versus  $\varepsilon_0$  for several values of  $\delta$ . here, the solid lines correspond to the robust minimax estimator and the dashed lines correspond to the lda estimator. figure 9(b) compares  $\mathsf{ra}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{\mathrm{lda}})$  with  $\mathsf{ra}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}^{\varepsilon})$  versus  $1 / \delta$  for various choices of  $\varepsilon_0$ . as we see for the case of  $p = 2$ , the lda has better robust accuracy and it is mostly very close to that of the robust estimator.  
-  $(p = \infty, q = 1)$ . similar to the setting of experiments in section 5.2, here we consider the scaling  $\varepsilon = \varepsilon_0 / \sqrt{d}$ . figure 10 (a) compares the robust accuracies versus  $\varepsilon_0$  for several values of  $\delta$ . as we see for any  $\delta$ , there exists  $\varepsilon_0^*(\delta)$  above which the robust minimax outperforms the lda.

![](images/f53e9db0b6963cfccc3d8b7746ef0d1fd1a1c6b7a556285d381ff247446c6bf3.jpg)  
figure 9: robust accuracies for the lda estimator and the robust minimax estimator versus the adversary's power with  $\ell_2(p = 2)$  perturbations for different values of  $\delta$ . solid curves correspond to the robust minimax estimator and the dashed curves correspond to the lda estimator.

![](images/d0dc50922f0ff1e697991f95d67414fab58947e10a2e764e8f38378928c38c52.jpg)

figure 10(b) compares the robust accuracies versus  $1 / \delta$  for several values of  $\varepsilon_0$ . rewording the above observation, for any  $\varepsilon_0$  there exists  $\delta^{*}(\varepsilon_{0})$  below which the robust minimax outperforms the lda estimator.

-  $(p = 1, q = \infty)$ . similar to the setting of experiments in section 5.3, we have  $\varepsilon = \varepsilon_0\|\pmb{\mu}\|_{\ell_p} = \sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}\frac{\varepsilon_0}{\sqrt{d}}$ . invoking equations (a.1), we have  $\lim_{n\to \infty}\mathsf{ra}(\widehat{\pmb{\mu}}^{\mathrm{lda}}) = 0$  because  $c_q = \sqrt{2\log d} \to \infty$ . however, as we see in figure 7, the robust minimax estimator  $\widehat{\pmb{\theta}}^\varepsilon$  achieves non-trivial positive robust accuracies and hence outperforms lda.

# b proofs for anisotropic gaussian model (section 6)

# b.1 proof of theorem 6.1

as discussed the  $(\varepsilon, q)$ -separability condition can alternatively be written as (3.2), which we repeat here:

$$
\exists \boldsymbol {\theta}, \| \boldsymbol {\theta} \| _ {\ell_ {q}} \leq \frac {1}{\varepsilon}: \quad \forall i \in [ n ], y _ {i} \langle \boldsymbol {x} _ {i}, \boldsymbol {\theta} \rangle > 1. \tag {b.1}
$$

to find the separability threshold we consider the following feasibility problem

$$
\min  _ {\boldsymbol {\theta} \in \mathbb {r} ^ {d}} 0 \quad \text {s u b j e c t} y _ {i} \left\langle \boldsymbol {x} _ {i}, \boldsymbol {\theta} \right\rangle > 1, \quad \| \boldsymbol {\theta} \| _ {\ell_ {q}} \leq \frac {1}{\varepsilon}. \tag {b.2}
$$

clearly this is a convex optimization problem since  $q \geq 1$ . writing the partial lagrangian for the above problem with  $u_{i} / n$  as dual coefficients, this is equivalent to

$$
\min  _ {\boldsymbol {\theta}} \max  _ {u _ {i} \geq 0} \frac {1}{n} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} u _ {i} \left(1 - y _ {i} \langle x _ {i}, \boldsymbol {\theta} \rangle\right) \quad \text {s u b j e c t} \quad \| \boldsymbol {\theta} \| _ {\ell_ {q}} \leq \frac {1}{\varepsilon}. \tag {b.3}
$$

![](images/feb0ddcac64b514759381de5836cab2b282e62b12b601bcc01dd8dd0ae64ea01.jpg)  
figure 10: robust accuracies for lda estimator and the robust minimax estimator versus the adversary's power with  $\ell_{\infty}$  ( $p = \infty$ ) perturbations for different values of  $\delta$ . solid curves correspond to the robust minimax estimator and the dashed curves correspond to the lda estimator.

![](images/c5f34a7245ec068644c7ae88210b0c7ada5fbab45a6bd8cd3016f80627c70ae4.jpg)

under our gaussian mixture data model, we can substitute for  $\pmb{x} = \pmb{y}\pmb{\mu}^{t} + \pmb{z}\pmb{\sigma}^{1/2}$ , which results in

$$
\min  _ {\boldsymbol {\theta}} \max  _ {u _ {i} \geq 0} \frac {1}{n} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {t} \left(\mathbf {1} \left(1 - \boldsymbol {\mu} ^ {t} \boldsymbol {\theta}\right) - d _ {\boldsymbol {y}} z \sigma^ {1 / 2} \boldsymbol {\theta}\right) \quad \text {s u b j e c t t o} \quad \| \boldsymbol {\theta} \| _ {\ell_ {q}} \leq \frac {1}{\varepsilon}. \tag {b.4}
$$

the above dual problem has a finite optimal value if and only if the data is  $(\varepsilon, q)$ -separable. so we aim at finding the largest  $\delta$  such that the above problem has still a finite optimal value. (recall that  $\frac{n}{d} \rightarrow \delta$ .)

reduction to an auxiliary optimization problem via cgmt. note that  $y_{i} = \pm 1$  are independent of  $z$ . in addition, the objective function in (b.4) is affine in the standard gaussian matrix  $z$  and the rest of the terms form a convex-concave function in  $\theta, u$ . due to this particular form we are able to apply a powerful extension of a classical gaussian process inequality due to gordon [gor88] known as convex gaussian minimax theorem (cgmt) [toh15], and is discussed in the proof sketch in section 7. the cgmt framework provides a principled machinery to characterize the asymptotic behavior of certain minimax optimization problems that are affine in a gaussian matrix  $x$ .

as discussed in the cgmt framework in section 7, we require minimization/maximization to be over compact sets. the vector  $\pmb{\theta}$  already lies in the  $\ell_q$  ball of radius  $1 / \varepsilon$  by constraint. in addition, since  $u_{i}\geq 0$ , and we are focused on the regime that (b.4) has finite optimal value, the optimal values of  $u_{i}$  should all be finite as well.

we are now ready to applying the cgmt framework. the corresponding auxiliary optimization (ao) reads as

$$
\min  _ {\boldsymbol {\theta}} \max  _ {\boldsymbol {u} \geq 0} \frac {1}{n} \left\{\left(\boldsymbol {u} ^ {t} \boldsymbol {1}\right) \left(1 - \boldsymbol {\mu} ^ {t} \boldsymbol {\theta}\right) + \left\| \boldsymbol {\sigma} ^ {1 / 2} \boldsymbol {\theta} \right\| _ {\ell_ {2}} \boldsymbol {g} ^ {t} \boldsymbol {u} + \| \boldsymbol {u} \| _ {\ell_ {2}} \boldsymbol {h} ^ {t} \boldsymbol {\sigma} ^ {1 / 2} \boldsymbol {\theta} \right\},
$$

$$
\text {s u b j e c t} \quad \| \boldsymbol {\theta} \| _ {\ell_ {q}} \leq \frac {1}{\varepsilon}, \tag {b.5}
$$

where  $\pmb{g} \sim \mathsf{n}(0, \pmb{i}_n)$  and  $\pmb{h} \sim \mathsf{n}(0, \pmb{i}_d)$ . fixing  $\beta := \frac{\|\pmb{u}\|_{\ell_2}}{\sqrt{n}}$  and optimizing over  $\pmb{u}$  on the non-negative

orthant we get

$$
\min  _ {\boldsymbol {\theta}} \max  _ {\beta \geq 0} \frac {\beta}{\sqrt {n}} \boldsymbol {h} ^ {t} \boldsymbol {\sigma} ^ {1 / 2} \boldsymbol {\theta} + \frac {\beta}{\sqrt {n}} \left\| \left(\left(1 - \boldsymbol {\mu} ^ {t} \boldsymbol {\theta}\right) \boldsymbol {1} + \left\| \boldsymbol {\sigma} ^ {1 / 2} \boldsymbol {\theta} \right\| _ {\ell_ {2}} \boldsymbol {g}\right) _ {+} \right\| _ {\ell_ {2}},
$$

$$
\text {s u b j e c t} \quad \| \boldsymbol {\theta} \| _ {\ell_ {q}} \leq \frac {1}{\varepsilon}. \tag {b.6}
$$

for data to be separable the above dual optimization should take finite optimal value and therefore the coefficient of  $\beta$  should be non-positive. as such the problem is separable if and only if the optimal value of the following problem is non-positive:

$$
\min _ {\pmb {\theta}} \frac {1}{\sqrt {n}} \pmb {h} ^ {t} \pmb {\sigma} ^ {1 / 2} \pmb {\theta} + \frac {1}{\sqrt {n}} \left\| \left(\left(1 - \pmb {\mu} ^ {t} \pmb {\theta}\right) \mathbf {1} + \left\| \pmb {\sigma} ^ {1 / 2} \pmb {\theta} \right\| _ {\ell_ {2}} \pmb {g}\right) _ {+} \right\| _ {\ell_ {2}} \leq 0,
$$

$$
\text {s u b j e c t} \quad \| \boldsymbol {\theta} \| _ {\ell_ {q}} \leq \frac {1}{\varepsilon}. \tag {b.7}
$$

consider the decomposition  $\pmb{\theta} = \pmb{\theta}_{\perp} + \theta \widetilde{\pmb{\mu}}$  with  $\pmb{\theta}_{\perp} = \mathsf{p}_{\pmb{\mu}}^{\perp}\pmb{\theta}$ . note that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \frac {1}{\sqrt {n}} \boldsymbol {h} ^ {t} \boldsymbol {\sigma} ^ {1 / 2} \boldsymbol {\theta} = \frac {1}{\sqrt {n}} \boldsymbol {h} ^ {t} \boldsymbol {\sigma} ^ {1 / 2} \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {\perp} + \frac {1}{\sqrt {n}} \theta \boldsymbol {h} ^ {t} \boldsymbol {\sigma} ^ {1 / 2} \widetilde {\boldsymbol {\mu}} \\ = \frac {1}{\sqrt {n}} \boldsymbol {h} ^ {t} \boldsymbol {\sigma} ^ {1 / 2} \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {\perp} + \frac {1}{\sqrt {n}} a \theta \boldsymbol {h} ^ {t} \widetilde {\boldsymbol {\mu}} \\ \end{array}
$$

since  $\pmb{h}^t\widetilde{\pmb{\mu}}\sim \mathsf{n}(0,1)$  and  $\theta$  is bounded the contribution of the second term is negligible in the large sample limit  $n\to \infty$ . this along with the symmetry of the distribution of  $\pmb{h}$  bring us to

$$
\begin{array}{l} \min _ {\alpha \geq 0, \theta , \boldsymbol {\theta}} - \frac {1}{\sqrt {n}} \boldsymbol {h} ^ {t} \boldsymbol {\sigma} ^ {1 / 2} \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {\perp} + \frac {1}{\sqrt {n}} \left\| \left(\left(1 - \| \boldsymbol {\mu} \| _ {\ell_ {2}} \theta\right) \mathbf {1} + \sqrt {\alpha^ {2} + a ^ {2} \theta^ {2}} \boldsymbol {g}\right) _ {+} \right\| _ {\ell_ {2}} \\ \text {s u b j e c t} \quad \| \boldsymbol {\theta} \| _ {\ell_ {q}} \leq \frac {1}{\varepsilon}, \quad \left\| \boldsymbol {\sigma} ^ {1 / 2} \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {\perp} \right\| _ {\ell_ {2}} = \alpha , \quad \widetilde {\boldsymbol {\mu}} ^ {t} \boldsymbol {\theta} = \theta \tag {b.8} \\ \end{array}
$$

scalarization of the auxiliary optimization problem. to continue recall the definition of set  $s$  given by

$$
\mathcal {s} (\alpha , \theta , \varepsilon_ {0}, \boldsymbol {\mu}) := \left\{\boldsymbol {z} \in \mathbb {r} ^ {d}: \quad \boldsymbol {z} ^ {t} \widetilde {\boldsymbol {\mu}} = 0, \| \boldsymbol {z} \| _ {\ell_ {2}} = \alpha , \left\| \boldsymbol {\sigma} ^ {- 1 / 2} \boldsymbol {z} + \theta \widetilde {\boldsymbol {\mu}} \right\| _ {\ell_ {q}} \leq \frac {1}{\varepsilon_ {0} \| \boldsymbol {\mu} \| _ {\ell_ {p}}} \right\}.
$$

recall that  $\varepsilon = \varepsilon_0\| \pmb {\mu}\|_{\ell_p}$  and so the optimization problem (b.8) above can be rewritten in the form

$$
\min  _ {\alpha \geq 0, \theta} \quad \min  _ {\boldsymbol {z} \in \mathcal {s} (\alpha , \theta , \varepsilon_ {0}, \boldsymbol {\mu})} - \frac {1}{\sqrt {n}} \boldsymbol {h} ^ {t} \boldsymbol {z} + \frac {1}{\sqrt {n}} \left\| \left(\left(1 - \| \boldsymbol {\mu} \| _ {\ell_ {2}} \theta\right) \mathbf {1} + \sqrt {\alpha^ {2} + a ^ {2} \theta^ {2}} \boldsymbol {g}\right) _ {+} \right\| _ {\ell_ {2}} \tag {b.9}
$$

recall the spherical width of a set  $\mathcal{s} \subset \mathbb{r}^d$  defined as

$$
\omega_ {s} \left(\mathcal {s}\right) = \mathbb {e} \left[ \sup _ {\boldsymbol {z} \in \mathcal {s}} \boldsymbol {z} ^ {t} \boldsymbol {u} \right],
$$

where  $\pmb{u} \in s^{d-1}$  is a vector chosen at random from the unit sphere. using this definition and the fact that  $\min z = -\max -z$  we have

$$
\min _ {\boldsymbol {z} \in \mathcal {s} (\alpha , \theta , \varepsilon_ {0})} - \frac {1}{\sqrt {n}} \boldsymbol {h} ^ {t} \boldsymbol {z} = - \frac {1}{\sqrt {n / d}} \sup _ {\boldsymbol {z} \in \mathcal {s} (\alpha , \theta , \varepsilon_ {0})} \frac {1}{\sqrt {d}} \boldsymbol {h} ^ {t} \boldsymbol {z} \to - \frac {1}{\sqrt {\delta}} \omega (\alpha , \theta , \varepsilon_ {0}),
$$

in probability, where in the last line we use the fact, for  $\mathcal{s} \in \mathbb{s}^{d - 1}$ , the function  $f(\pmb{u}) = \sup_{\pmb{z} \in \mathcal{s}} \pmb{z}^t \pmb{u}$  is lipschitz. therefore, using the concentration of lipschitz functions of gaussian random vectors (see e.g. [ver18, theorem 5.2.2]),  $f(\pmb{u})$  concentrates around its mean  $\mathbb{e} f(\pmb{u}) = \omega_s (\mathcal{s}(\alpha, \theta, \varepsilon_0, \pmb{\mu}))$ . more precisely,

$$
\mathbb {p} \left\{\left| \sup  _ {\boldsymbol {z} \in \mathcal {s}} \frac {1}{\sqrt {d}} \boldsymbol {h} ^ {t} \boldsymbol {z} - \omega_ {s} (\mathcal {s} (\alpha , \theta , \varepsilon_ {0}, \boldsymbol {\mu})) \right| \right\} \leq 2 e ^ {- c d t ^ {2}},
$$

for an absolute constant  $c > 0$  and for every  $t \geq 0$ . therefore, by invoking the assumption on the convergence of spherical width, cf. assumption 3, we arrive at

$$
\lim _ {d \to \infty} \mathbb {p} \left\{\left| \sup _ {\boldsymbol {z} \in \mathcal {s}} \frac {1}{\sqrt {d}} \boldsymbol {h} ^ {t} \boldsymbol {z} - \omega (\alpha , \theta , \varepsilon_ {0}) \right| \geq \eta \right\} = 0, \quad \forall \eta > 0.
$$

therefore,  $\sup_{\boldsymbol{z} \in \mathcal{s}(\alpha, \theta, \varepsilon_0)} \frac{1}{\sqrt{d}} \boldsymbol{h}^t \boldsymbol{z} \to \omega(\alpha, \theta, \varepsilon_0)$ , in probability.

furthermore,  $\| \pmb{\mu}\|_{\ell_2} \to v$  by assumption 2 and since  $\pmb{g} \sim \mathsf{n}(0, i_n)$  by applying the weak law of large numbers we have

$$
\frac {1}{\sqrt {n}} \left\| \left(\left(1 - \| \pmb {\mu} \| _ {\ell_ {2}} \theta\right) \mathbf {1} + \sqrt {\alpha^ {2} + a ^ {2} \theta^ {2}} \mathbf {\nabla} g\right) _ {+} \right\| _ {\ell_ {2}} \to \sqrt {\mathbb {e} \left[ \left(1 - v \theta + \sqrt {\alpha^ {2} + a ^ {2} \theta^ {2}} \mathbf {\nabla} g\right) _ {+} ^ {2} \right]}
$$

thus the objective function in the optimization problem (b.9) converges pointwise to

$$
\min  _ {\alpha \geq 0, \theta} - \frac {1}{\sqrt {\delta}} \omega (\alpha , \theta , \varepsilon_ {0}) + \sqrt {\mathbb {e} \left[ \left(1 - v \theta + \sqrt {\alpha^ {2} + a ^ {2} \theta^ {2}} g\right) _ {+} ^ {2} \right]} \tag {b.10}
$$

also the problem (b.9) is convex as a function of  $(\alpha, \theta, z)$  and since partial maximization preserves convexity, the objective of (b.9) (after minimization over  $z$ ) is a convex function of  $(\alpha, \theta)$ . we can thus apply the convexity lemma [tah18, lemma b.2] to conclude that the minimum value of (b.9) over  $\alpha \geq 0, \theta$  also converges to that of (b.10). therefore, we conclude that data is  $(\varepsilon, q)$ -separable if and only if the optimal value in (b.10) is finite. rearranging the terms gives us that (b.10) has a finite optimal value if and only if

$$
\delta <   \delta_ {*}, \quad \mathrm {w i t h} \delta_ {*} := \min _ {\alpha \geq 0, \theta} \frac {\omega (\alpha , \theta , \varepsilon_ {0}) ^ {2}}{\mathbb {e} \left[ (1 - v \theta + \sqrt {\alpha^ {2} + \theta^ {2}} g) _ {+} ^ {2} \right]}. \tag {b.11}
$$

this completes the proof of theorem 6.1.

# b.2 proof of theorem 6.3

we prove theorem 6.3 using the convex gaussian minimax theorem (cgmt) as outlined in section 7. the max-margin problem (f.2) can be equivalently written as

$$
\left(\widetilde {\boldsymbol {\theta}} ^ {\varepsilon}, \widehat {\gamma}\right) = \arg \min  _ {\boldsymbol {\theta}, \gamma \geq 0} \| \boldsymbol {\theta} \| _ {\ell_ {2}} ^ {2} \tag {b.12}
$$

$$
\mathrm {s u b j e c t t o} y _ {i} \langle \pmb {x} _ {i}, \pmb {\theta} \rangle - \varepsilon \gamma \geq 1, \quad \gamma \geq \| \pmb {\theta} \| _ {\ell_ {q}}
$$

now note that writing the lagrangian for the max-margin problem with  $u_{i} / n$  and  $2\lambda$  as dual coefficients, this is equivalent to

$$
\min  _ {\boldsymbol {\theta}, \gamma \geq 0} \max  _ {u _ {i}, \lambda \geq 0} \| \boldsymbol {\theta} \| _ {\ell_ {2}} ^ {2} + \frac {1}{n} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} u _ {i} \left(1 + \varepsilon \gamma - y _ {i} \langle x _ {i}, \boldsymbol {\theta} \rangle\right) + 2 \lambda \left(\| \boldsymbol {\theta} \| _ {\ell_ {q}} - \gamma\right). \tag {b.13}
$$

we next substitute for  $\pmb{x} = \pmb{y}\pmb{\mu}^{t} + \pmb{z}\pmb{\sigma}^{1/2}$  based on the gaussian mixtures model to arrive at

$$
\min _ {\boldsymbol {\theta}, \gamma \geq 0} \max _ {u _ {i} \geq 0, \lambda \geq 0} \| \boldsymbol {\theta} \| _ {\ell_ {2}} ^ {2} + \frac {1}{n} \left(\boldsymbol {u} ^ {t} \mathbf {1} + \varepsilon \gamma \boldsymbol {u} ^ {t} \mathbf {1} - \boldsymbol {u} ^ {t} \boldsymbol {d _ {y}} \boldsymbol {z} \boldsymbol {\sigma} ^ {1 / 2} \boldsymbol {\theta} - \boldsymbol {u} ^ {t} \mathbf {1} \boldsymbol {\mu} ^ {t} \boldsymbol {\theta}\right) + 2 \lambda \left(\| \boldsymbol {\theta} \| _ {\ell_ {q}} - \gamma\right). \tag {b.14}
$$

the advantage of the lagrangian form in (b.14) is that it is a minimax problem and the objective is an affine function of the standard gaussian matrix  $\mathbf{z}$ . therefore, we can deploy the convex gaussian minimax theorem (cgmt) [toh15], described in section 7, to characterize asymptotic values of certain functions of this optimization solution, in a high probability sense.

to recall, the cgmt framework shows that a problem of the form

$$
\min  _ {\boldsymbol {\theta} \in \mathcal {s} _ {\boldsymbol {\theta}}} \max  _ {\boldsymbol {u} \in \mathcal {s} _ {\boldsymbol {u}}} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {t} \boldsymbol {z} \boldsymbol {\theta} + \psi (\boldsymbol {\theta}, \boldsymbol {u}) \tag {b.15}
$$

with  $z$  a matrix with  $\mathcal{n}(0,1)$  entries can be replaced asymptotically with

$$
\min  _ {\boldsymbol {\theta} \in \mathcal {s} _ {\boldsymbol {\theta}}} \max  _ {\boldsymbol {u} \in \mathcal {s} _ {\boldsymbol {u}}} \| \boldsymbol {\theta} \| _ {\ell_ {2}} \boldsymbol {g} ^ {t} \boldsymbol {u} + \| \boldsymbol {u} \| _ {\ell_ {2}} \boldsymbol {h} ^ {t} \boldsymbol {\theta} + \psi (\boldsymbol {\theta}, \boldsymbol {u}) \tag {b.16}
$$

where  $\pmb{g}$  and  $\pmb{h}$  are independent gaussian vectors with i.i.d.  $\mathcal{n}(0,1)$  entries and  $\psi(\theta, \pmb{u})$  is convex in  $\pmb{\theta}$  and concave in  $\pmb{u}$ . specifically, the optimal value and corresponding solution of (b.15) converge in probability to the optimal value and the corresponding solution of (b.16). in the above  $s_{\theta}$  and  $s_{\pmb{u}}$  are compact sets. we refer to [toh15, theorem 3] for precise statements. as explained in the proof sketch in 7, we follow [toh15] in referring to problems of the form (b.15) and (b.16) as the primal problem (po) and the auxiliary problem (ao).

note that in order to apply cgmt, we need the minimization/maximization to be over compact sets. this technical issue can be avoided by introducing "artificial" boundedness constraints on the optimization variables that they do not change the optimal solution. concretely, we can add constraints of the form  $\mathcal{s}_{\pmb{\theta}} = \{\pmb{\theta} : \| \pmb{\theta}\|_{\ell_q} \leq k_{\pmb{\theta}}\}$  and  $\mathcal{s}_{\pmb{u}} = \{\pmb{u} : 0 \leq u_i, \frac{1}{n}\pmb{1}^t\pmb{u} \leq k_{\pmb{u}}\}$  for sufficiently large constants  $k_{\pmb{\theta}}$ ,  $k_{\pmb{u}}$  without changing the optimal solution of (b.14) in a precise asymptotic sense. we refer to appendix e.3.1 for precise statements and proofs. this allows us to replace (b.14) with

$$
\min  _ {\boldsymbol {\theta} \in \mathcal {s} _ {\boldsymbol {\theta}}, \gamma \geq 0} \max  _ {\boldsymbol {u} \in \mathcal {s} _ {\boldsymbol {u}}, \lambda \geq 0} \| \boldsymbol {\theta} \| _ {\ell_ {2}} ^ {2} + \frac {1}{n} \left(\boldsymbol {u} ^ {t} \boldsymbol {1} + \varepsilon \gamma \boldsymbol {u} ^ {t} \boldsymbol {1} - \boldsymbol {u} ^ {t} d _ {\boldsymbol {y}} z \sigma^ {1 / 2} \boldsymbol {\theta} - \boldsymbol {u} ^ {t} \boldsymbol {1} \boldsymbol {\mu} ^ {t} \boldsymbol {\theta}\right) + 2 \lambda \left(\| \boldsymbol {\theta} \| _ {\ell_ {q}} - \gamma\right). \tag {b.17}
$$

reduction to an auxiliary optimization problem via cgmt. with these compact constraints in place we can now apply the cgmt result to obtain the auxiliary optimization (ao) problem.

we proceed by defining the projection matrices

$$
\mathsf {p} _ {\mu} ^ {\perp} := \boldsymbol {i} - \widetilde {\mu} \widetilde {\mu} ^ {t}, \quad \mathsf {p} _ {\mu} := \widetilde {\mu} \widetilde {\mu} ^ {t}
$$

and rewrite  $z\sigma^{1/2} = z\left(\mathsf{p}_{\mu} + \mathsf{p}_{\mu}^{\perp}\right)\sigma^{1/2}$ . since  $z\mathsf{p}_{\mu}$  and  $z\mathsf{p}_{\mu}^{\perp}$  are independent from each other the latter has the same distribution as

$$
\boldsymbol {z} \boldsymbol {\sigma} ^ {1 / 2} \sim \boldsymbol {z} \left(\boldsymbol {\sigma} ^ {1 / 2} \widetilde {\boldsymbol {\mu}}\right) ^ {t} + \boldsymbol {z} \mathsf {p} _ {\boldsymbol {\mu}} ^ {\perp} \boldsymbol {\sigma} ^ {1 / 2}
$$

where  $\mathbf{z} \sim \mathcal{n}(0, i_n)$  and is independent from the matrix  $\mathbf{z}$ . this brings us to the following representation

$$
\begin{array}{l} \min  _ {\boldsymbol {\theta}, \gamma \geq 0} \max  _ {\boldsymbol {u} \geq 0, \lambda \geq 0} \| \boldsymbol {\theta} \| _ {\ell_ {2}} ^ {2} + 2 \lambda \left(\| \boldsymbol {\theta} \| _ {\ell_ {q}} - \gamma\right) \\ + \frac {1}{n} \left\{\pmb {u} ^ {t} \pmb {1} + \varepsilon \gamma \pmb {u} ^ {t} \pmb {1} - \pmb {u} ^ {t} \pmb {1} \pmb {\mu} ^ {t} \pmb {\theta} - (\pmb {u} ^ {\top} d _ {y} z) (\widetilde {\pmb {\mu}} ^ {t} \pmb {\sigma} ^ {1 / 2} \pmb {\theta}) - \pmb {u} ^ {\top} d _ {y} z \mathsf {p} _ {\mu} ^ {\perp} \pmb {\sigma} ^ {1 / 2} \pmb {\theta} \right\} \\ \end{array}
$$

since  $y_{i} = \pm 1$  are independent of  $z$ , by applying cgmt framework, the ao reads as

$$
\begin{array}{l} \min  _ {\boldsymbol {\theta}, \gamma \geq 0} \max  _ {\boldsymbol {u} \geq 0, \lambda \geq 0} \| \boldsymbol {\theta} \| _ {\ell_ {2}} ^ {2} + 2 \lambda \left(\| \boldsymbol {\theta} \| _ {\ell_ {q}} - \gamma\right) + \frac {1}{n} \left\{\boldsymbol {u} ^ {t} \boldsymbol {1} + \varepsilon \gamma \boldsymbol {u} ^ {t} \boldsymbol {1} - \boldsymbol {u} ^ {t} \boldsymbol {1} \boldsymbol {\mu} ^ {t} \boldsymbol {\theta} \right. \\ + \left(\boldsymbol {u} ^ {t} \boldsymbol {z}\right) \left(\widetilde {\boldsymbol {\mu}} ^ {t} \boldsymbol {\sigma} ^ {1 / 2} \boldsymbol {\theta}\right) + \left\| \mathrm {p} _ {\boldsymbol {\mu}} ^ {\perp} \boldsymbol {\sigma} ^ {1 / 2} \boldsymbol {\theta} \right\| _ {\ell_ {2}} \boldsymbol {g} ^ {t} \boldsymbol {u} \\ \left. + \left\| \boldsymbol {u} \right\| _ {\ell_ {2}} \boldsymbol {h} ^ {t} \mathsf {p} _ {\boldsymbol {\mu}} ^ {\perp} \boldsymbol {\sigma} ^ {1 / 2} \boldsymbol {\theta} \right\} \\ \end{array}
$$

fixing  $\beta \coloneqq \frac{\|\pmb{u}\|_{\ell_2}}{\sqrt{n}}$  and optimizing over  $\pmb{u}$  on the non-negative orthant we get

$$
\begin{array}{l} \min  _ {\boldsymbol {\theta}, \gamma \geq 0} \max  _ {\beta \geq 0, \lambda \geq 0} \quad \| \boldsymbol {\theta} \| _ {\ell_ {2}} ^ {2} + 2 \lambda \left(\| \boldsymbol {\theta} \| _ {\ell_ {q}} - \gamma\right) + \frac {\beta}{\sqrt {n}} \boldsymbol {h} ^ {t} \mathsf {p} _ {\mu} ^ {\perp} \sigma^ {1 / 2} \boldsymbol {\theta} \\ + \frac {\beta}{\sqrt {n}} \left\| \left(\left(1 + \varepsilon \gamma - \boldsymbol {\mu} ^ {t} \boldsymbol {\theta}\right) \mathbf {1} + \left(\widetilde {\boldsymbol {\mu}} ^ {t} \boldsymbol {\sigma} ^ {1 / 2} \boldsymbol {\theta}\right) \boldsymbol {z} + \left\| \mathrm {p} _ {\boldsymbol {\mu}} ^ {\perp} \boldsymbol {\sigma} ^ {1 / 2} \boldsymbol {\theta} \right\| _ {\ell_ {2}} \boldsymbol {g}\right) _ {+} \right\| _ {\ell_ {2}} \tag {b.18} \\ \end{array}
$$

since  $\mathbf{z}, \mathbf{g} \sim \mathsf{n}(0, i_n)$  are independent, by applying the weak law of large numbers we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} \frac {1}{\sqrt {n}} \left\| \left(\left(1 + \varepsilon \gamma - \boldsymbol {\mu} ^ {t} \boldsymbol {\theta}\right) \mathbf {1} + (\widetilde {\boldsymbol {\mu}} ^ {t} \boldsymbol {\sigma} ^ {1 / 2} \boldsymbol {\theta}) \mathbf {z} + \left\| \mathsf {p} _ {\boldsymbol {\mu}} ^ {\perp} \boldsymbol {\sigma} ^ {1 / 2} \boldsymbol {\theta} \right\| _ {\ell_ {2}} \mathbf {g}\right) _ {+} \right\| _ {\ell_ {2}} \\ \rightarrow \left(\mathbb {e} \left[\left(\left(1 + \varepsilon \gamma - \boldsymbol {\mu} ^ {t} \boldsymbol {\theta}\right) + \sqrt {(\widetilde {\boldsymbol {\mu}} ^ {t} \boldsymbol {\sigma} ^ {1 / 2} \boldsymbol {\theta}) ^ {2} + \left\| \mathsf {p} _ {\boldsymbol {\mu}} ^ {\perp} \boldsymbol {\sigma} ^ {1 / 2} \boldsymbol {\theta} \right\| _ {\ell_ {2}} ^ {2}} g\right) _ {+} ^ {2} \right]\right) ^ {\frac {1}{2}} \\ = \left(\mathbb {e} \left[ \left(\left(1 + \varepsilon \gamma - \boldsymbol {\mu} ^ {t} \boldsymbol {\theta}\right) + \left\| \boldsymbol {\sigma} ^ {\frac {1}{2}} \boldsymbol {\theta} \right\| _ {\ell_ {2}} g\right) _ {+} ^ {2} \right]\right) ^ {\frac {1}{2}} \\ \end{array}
$$

thus we arrive at

$$
\begin{array}{l} \min  _ {\boldsymbol {\theta}, \gamma \geq 0} \max  _ {\beta \geq 0, \lambda \geq 0} \| \boldsymbol {\theta} \| _ {\ell_ {2}} ^ {2} + 2 \lambda \left(\| \boldsymbol {\theta} \| _ {\ell_ {q}} - \gamma\right) + \frac {\beta}{\sqrt {n}} \boldsymbol {h} ^ {t} \mathsf {p} _ {\boldsymbol {\mu}} ^ {\perp} \sigma^ {1 / 2} \boldsymbol {\theta} \\ + \beta \sqrt {\mathbb {e} \left[ \left(\left(1 + \varepsilon \gamma - \boldsymbol {\mu} ^ {t} \boldsymbol {\theta}\right) + \left\| \boldsymbol {\sigma} ^ {\frac {1}{2}} \boldsymbol {\theta} \right\| _ {\ell_ {2}} g\right) _ {+} ^ {2} \right]}. \tag {b.19} \\ \end{array}
$$

we note that for  $a\geq 0$

$$
\mathbb {e} \bigl [ a g + b \bigr ] _ {+} ^ {2} = \frac {a ^ {2} + b ^ {2}}{2} \left(1 + \mathrm {e r f} \left(\frac {b}{\sqrt {2} a}\right)\right) + \frac {a b}{\sqrt {2 \pi}} e ^ {- \frac {b ^ {2}}{2 a ^ {2}}}.
$$

and its derivative with respect to  $a$  is given by  $2a(1 + \operatorname{erf}\left(\frac{b}{\sqrt{2a}}\right)) > 0$  which implies that the function is increasing in  $a > 0$ . therefore the optimization (b.19) can be equivalently written as

$$
\min_{\boldsymbol {\theta},\gamma ,\alpha \geq 0}\max_{\beta \geq 0,\lambda \geq 0}\quad \| \boldsymbol {\theta}\|_{\ell_{2}}^{2} + 2\lambda \left(\| \boldsymbol {\theta}\|_{\ell_{q}} - \gamma\right) + \frac{\beta}{\sqrt{n}}\boldsymbol{h}^{t}\mathsf{p}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{\perp}\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{1 / 2}\boldsymbol {\theta} + \beta \sqrt{\mathbb{e}\left[((1 + \varepsilon\gamma - \boldsymbol{\mu}^{t}\boldsymbol{\theta}) + \alpha g)_{+}^{2}\right]}
$$

$$
\text {s u b j e c t} \quad \left\| \boldsymbol {\sigma} ^ {\frac {1}{2}} \boldsymbol {\theta} \right\| _ {\ell_ {2}} \leq \alpha . \tag {b.20}
$$

note that the above is trivially jointly convex in  $(\pmb{\theta}, \gamma, \alpha)$  and jointly concave in  $(\beta, \lambda)$ . we fix the parallel component of  $\pmb{\theta}$  on  $\pmb{\mu}$  to  $\theta$ , namely  $\theta = \widetilde{\pmb{\mu}}^t\pmb{\theta}$ . we next optimize over  $\pmb{\theta}$  while fixing  $\theta$ .

$$
\min_{\theta ,\boldsymbol {\theta},\gamma \geq 0,\alpha \geq 0}\max_{\beta \geq 0,\lambda \geq 0}\quad \| \boldsymbol {\theta}\|_{\ell_{2}}^{2} + 2\lambda \left(\| \boldsymbol {\theta}\|_{\ell_{q}} - \gamma\right) + \frac{\beta}{\sqrt{n}}\boldsymbol{h}^{t}\mathsf{p}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{\perp}\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{1 / 2}\boldsymbol {\theta} + \beta \sqrt{\mathbb{e}\left[\left(\left(1 + \varepsilon\gamma - \theta\left\|\boldsymbol{\mu}\right\|_{\ell_{2}}\right) + \alpha g\right)_{+}^{2}\right]}
$$

$$
\text {s u b j e c t} \quad \left\| \boldsymbol {\sigma} ^ {\frac {1}{2}} \boldsymbol {\theta} \right\| _ {\ell_ {2}} \leq \alpha , \quad \widetilde {\boldsymbol {\mu}} ^ {t} \boldsymbol {\theta} = \theta \tag {b.21}
$$

bringing the constraints into the objective via lagrange multipliers we obtain

$$
\begin{array}{l} \min_{\theta ,\boldsymbol {\theta},\gamma \geq 0,\alpha \geq 0}\max_{\beta ,\lambda ,\eta \geq 0,\widetilde{\eta}}\quad \| \boldsymbol {\theta}\|_{\ell_{2}}^{2} + 2\lambda \left(\| \boldsymbol {\theta}\|_{\ell_{q}} - \gamma\right) + \frac{\beta}{\sqrt{n}}\boldsymbol{h}^{t}\mathsf{p}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{\perp}\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{1 / 2}\boldsymbol {\theta} + \beta \sqrt{\mathbb{e}\left[\left(\left(1 + \varepsilon\gamma - \theta\left\|\boldsymbol{\mu}\right\|_{\ell_{2}}\right) + \alpha g\right)_{+}^{2}\right]} \\ + \eta \left(\left\| \boldsymbol {\sigma} ^ {\frac {1}{2}} \boldsymbol {\theta} \right\| _ {\ell_ {2}} - \alpha\right) + \widetilde {\eta} (\widetilde {\boldsymbol {\mu}} ^ {t} \boldsymbol {\theta} - \theta) \tag {b.22} \\ \end{array}
$$

$$
\mathrm {n e x t n o t e t h a t} \left\| \boldsymbol {\sigma} ^ {\frac {1}{2}} \boldsymbol {\theta} \right\| _ {\ell_ {2}} = \min _ {\tau \geq 0} \frac {\left\| \boldsymbol {\sigma} ^ {\frac {1}{2}} \boldsymbol {\theta} \right\| _ {\ell_ {2}} ^ {2}}{2 \tau} + \frac {\tau}{2} \mathrm {a n d} \| \boldsymbol {\theta} \| _ {\ell_ {q}} = \min _ {t \geq 0} \frac {\left\| \boldsymbol {\theta} \right\| _ {\ell_ {q}} ^ {q}}{q t ^ {q - 1}} + \frac {q - 1}{q} t
$$

thus, above reduces to

$$
\begin{array}{l} \min  _ {\theta , \boldsymbol {\theta}, \gamma \geq 0, \alpha \geq 0} \max  _ {\beta , \lambda , \eta \geq 0, \widetilde {\eta}} \min  _ {\tau \geq 0, t \geq 0} \| \boldsymbol {\theta} \| _ {\ell_ {2}} ^ {2} + \frac {2 \lambda}{q t ^ {q - 1}} \| \boldsymbol {\theta} \| _ {\ell_ {q}} ^ {q} + 2 \lambda \frac {q - 1}{q} t - 2 \lambda \gamma + \frac {\beta}{\sqrt {n}} \boldsymbol {h} ^ {t} \mathsf {p} _ {\boldsymbol {\mu}} ^ {\perp} \boldsymbol {\sigma} ^ {1 / 2} \boldsymbol {\theta} \\ + \beta \sqrt {\mathbb {e} \left[ \left(\left(1 + \varepsilon \gamma - \theta \| \boldsymbol {\mu} \| _ {\ell_ {2}}\right) + \alpha g\right) _ {+} ^ {2} \right]} \\ + \frac {\eta}{2 \tau} \left\| \boldsymbol {\sigma} ^ {\frac {1}{2}} \boldsymbol {\theta} \right\| _ {\ell_ {2}} ^ {2} + \frac {\eta \tau}{2} - \eta \alpha + \widetilde {\eta} (\widetilde {\boldsymbol {\mu}} ^ {t} \boldsymbol {\theta} - \theta) \tag {b.23} \\ \end{array}
$$

to continue note that  $\frac{\|\pmb{\theta}\|_{\ell_q}^q}{t^{q - 1}} = t\left\| \frac{\pmb{\theta}}{t}\right\|_{\ell_q}^q$  and  $\frac{\left\|\sigma^{\frac{1}{2}}\pmb{\theta}\right\|_{\ell_2}^2}{\tau} = \tau \left\| \sigma^{\frac{1}{2}}\frac{\pmb{\theta}}{\tau}\right\|_{\ell_2}^2$  and thus using the fact that the perspective of a convex function is convex both are jointly convex with respect to  $(\pmb {\theta},t)$  and  $(\pmb {\theta},\tau)$ . thus the objective above is jointly convex in  $(\pmb {\theta},\pmb {\theta},\gamma ,\alpha ,t,\tau)$  and jointly concave in  $(\beta ,\lambda ,\eta ,\widetilde{\eta})$ . due to this convexity/concavity with respect to the minimization/maximization parameters we can change the order of min and max. we thus proceed by optimizing over  $\pmb{\theta}$ . the optimization over  $\pmb{\theta}$  takes the form

$$
\min  _ {\boldsymbol {\theta}} \quad \| \boldsymbol {\theta} \| _ {\ell_ {2}} ^ {2} + \frac {2 \lambda}{q t ^ {q - 1}} \| \boldsymbol {\theta} \| _ {\ell_ {q}} ^ {q} + \frac {\eta}{2 \tau} \left\| \boldsymbol {\sigma} ^ {\frac {1}{2}} \boldsymbol {\theta} \right\| _ {\ell_ {2}} ^ {2} + \frac {\beta}{\sqrt {n}} \boldsymbol {h} ^ {t} \mathrm {p} _ {\boldsymbol {\mu}} ^ {\perp} \boldsymbol {\sigma} ^ {1 / 2} \boldsymbol {\theta} + \widetilde {\eta} \widetilde {\boldsymbol {\mu}} ^ {t} \boldsymbol {\theta} \tag {b.24}
$$

by completing the square the objective can be alternatively written as

$$
\begin{array}{l} \boldsymbol {\theta} ^ {t} \left(\boldsymbol {i} + \frac {\eta}{2 \tau} \boldsymbol {\sigma}\right) \boldsymbol {\theta} + \frac {2 \lambda}{q t ^ {q - 1}} \left\| \boldsymbol {\theta} \right\| _ {\ell_ {q}} ^ {q} + \frac {\beta}{\sqrt {n}} \boldsymbol {h} ^ {t} \mathsf {p} _ {\boldsymbol {\mu}} ^ {\perp} \boldsymbol {\sigma} ^ {1 / 2} \boldsymbol {\theta} + \widetilde {\eta} \widetilde {\boldsymbol {\mu}} ^ {t} \boldsymbol {\theta} \\ = \left\| \left(\boldsymbol {i} + \frac {\eta}{2 \tau} \boldsymbol {\sigma}\right) ^ {1 / 2} \boldsymbol {\theta} + \frac {\beta}{2 \sqrt {n}} \left(\boldsymbol {i} + \frac {\eta}{2 \tau} \boldsymbol {\sigma}\right) ^ {- 1 / 2} \boldsymbol {\sigma} ^ {1 / 2} \mathsf {p} _ {\mu} ^ {\perp} \boldsymbol {h} + \left(\boldsymbol {i} + \frac {\eta}{2 \tau} \boldsymbol {\sigma}\right) ^ {- 1 / 2} \frac {\tilde {\eta}}{2} \tilde {\mu} \right\| _ {\ell_ {2}} ^ {2} \\ + \frac {2 \lambda}{q t ^ {q - 1}} \| \boldsymbol {\theta} \| _ {\ell_ {q}} ^ {q} - \frac {\beta^ {2}}{4 n} \boldsymbol {h} ^ {t} \mathsf {p} _ {\mu} ^ {\perp} \boldsymbol {\sigma} ^ {1 / 2} \left(\boldsymbol {i} + \frac {\eta}{2 \tau} \boldsymbol {\sigma}\right) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {\sigma} ^ {1 / 2} \mathsf {p} _ {\mu} ^ {\perp} \boldsymbol {h} \\ - \frac {\tilde {\eta} ^ {2}}{4} \widetilde {\boldsymbol {\mu}} ^ {t} \left(\boldsymbol {i} + \frac {\eta}{2 \tau} \boldsymbol {\sigma}\right) ^ {- 1} \widetilde {\boldsymbol {\mu}} - \frac {\beta \tilde {\eta}}{2 \sqrt {n}} \widetilde {\boldsymbol {\mu}} ^ {t} \left(\boldsymbol {i} + \frac {\eta}{2 \tau} \boldsymbol {\sigma}\right) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {\sigma} ^ {1 / 2} \mathsf {p} _ {\boldsymbol {\mu}} ^ {\perp} \boldsymbol {h}. \tag {b.25} \\ \end{array}
$$

since  $\pmb{\sigma}\widetilde{\pmb{\mu}} = a^{2}\widetilde{\pmb{\mu}}$  we have

$$
\widetilde {\boldsymbol {\mu}} ^ {t} \left(\boldsymbol {i} + \frac {\eta}{2 \tau} \boldsymbol {\sigma}\right) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {\sigma} ^ {1 / 2} \mathrm {p} _ {\boldsymbol {\mu}} ^ {\perp} \boldsymbol {h} = 0, \quad \widetilde {\boldsymbol {\mu}} ^ {t} \left(\boldsymbol {i} + \frac {\eta}{2 \tau} \boldsymbol {\sigma}\right) ^ {- 1} \widetilde {\boldsymbol {\mu}} = \frac {1}{\left(1 + \frac {\eta}{2 \tau} a ^ {2}\right)}.
$$

we consider a singular value decomposition  $\pmb{\sigma} = \pmb{u}\pmb{s}\pmb{u}^t$  with  $\pmb{s} = \mathrm{diag}(s_1,\dots,s_d)$ , and the first column of  $\pmb{u}$  being  $\widetilde{\pmb{\mu}}$  and  $s_1 = a^2$  (recall that  $\widetilde{\pmb{\mu}}$  is a singular value of  $\pmb{\sigma}$  with eigenvalue  $a^2$ .) then,

$$
\begin{array}{l} \frac {1}{n} \boldsymbol {h} ^ {t} \mathsf {p} _ {\mu} ^ {\perp} \boldsymbol {\sigma} ^ {1 / 2} \left(\boldsymbol {i} + \frac {\eta}{2 \tau} \boldsymbol {\sigma}\right) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {\sigma} ^ {1 / 2} \mathsf {p} _ {\mu} ^ {\perp} \boldsymbol {h} = \frac {1}{n} \boldsymbol {h} ^ {t} \mathsf {p} _ {\mu} ^ {\perp} \boldsymbol {u} \left(\boldsymbol {i} + \frac {\eta}{2 \tau} \boldsymbol {s}\right) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {s} \boldsymbol {u} ^ {t} \mathsf {p} _ {\mu} ^ {\perp} \boldsymbol {h} \\ = \frac {1}{\delta d} \sum_ {i = 2} ^ {d} \frac {s _ {i}}{1 + \frac {\eta}{2 \tau} s _ {i}} h _ {i} ^ {2} \\ \stackrel {p} {\rightarrow} \frac {1}{\delta d} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {d} \frac {s _ {i}}{1 + \frac {\eta}{2 \tau} s _ {i}} \\ = \frac {2 \tau}{\delta d \eta} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {d} \left(1 - \frac {1}{\frac {\eta}{2 \tau} \left(s _ {i} + \frac {2 \tau}{\eta}\right)}\right) \\ = \frac {2 \tau}{\delta \eta} \left(1 + \frac {2 \tau}{\eta} s _ {\rho} \left(- \frac {2 \tau}{\eta}\right)\right) \\ \end{array}
$$

with  $s_{\rho}(z) \coloneqq \int \frac{\rho(t)}{z - t} \, \mathrm{d}t$  the stieltjes transform of the spectrum of  $\pmb{\sigma}$ .

using the above identities (b.25) reduces to

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left\| \left(\boldsymbol {i} + \frac {\eta}{2 \tau} \boldsymbol {\sigma}\right) ^ {1 / 2} \boldsymbol {\theta} + \frac {\beta}{2 \sqrt {n}} \left(\boldsymbol {i} + \frac {\eta}{2 \tau} \boldsymbol {\sigma}\right) ^ {- 1 / 2} \boldsymbol {\sigma} ^ {1 / 2} \mathsf {p} _ {\mu} ^ {\perp} \boldsymbol {h} + \left(\boldsymbol {i} + \frac {\eta}{2 \tau} \boldsymbol {\sigma}\right) ^ {- 1 / 2} \frac {\tilde {\eta}}{2} \tilde {\boldsymbol {\mu}} \right\| _ {\ell_ {2}} ^ {2} \\ + \frac {2 \lambda}{q t ^ {q - 1}} \left\| \pmb {\theta} \right\| _ {\ell_ {q}} ^ {q} - \frac {\tau \beta^ {2}}{2 \delta \eta} \left(1 + \frac {2 \tau}{\eta} s _ {\rho} \left(- \frac {2 \tau}{\eta}\right)\right) - \frac {\tilde {\eta} ^ {2}}{4 (1 + \frac {\eta}{2 \tau} a ^ {2})}. \\ \end{array}
$$

we then write the minimum value over  $\pmb{\theta}$  in terms of the weighted moreau envelope, given by definition 6.2.

$$
\begin{array}{l} \min  _ {\boldsymbol {\theta}} \left\| \left(\boldsymbol {i} + \frac {\eta}{2 \tau} \boldsymbol {\sigma}\right) ^ {1 / 2} \boldsymbol {\theta} + \frac {\beta}{2 \sqrt {n}} \left(\boldsymbol {i} + \frac {\eta}{2 \tau} \boldsymbol {\sigma}\right) ^ {- 1 / 2} \boldsymbol {\sigma} ^ {1 / 2} \mathrm {p} _ {\mu} ^ {\perp} \boldsymbol {h} + \left(\boldsymbol {i} + \frac {\eta}{2 \tau} \boldsymbol {\sigma}\right) ^ {- 1 / 2} \frac {\tilde {\eta}}{2} \widetilde {\boldsymbol {\mu}} \right\| _ {\ell_ {2}} ^ {2} + \frac {2 \lambda}{q t ^ {q - 1}} \| \boldsymbol {\theta} \| _ {\ell_ {q}} ^ {q} \\ = 2 e _ {q, \boldsymbol {i} + \frac {\eta}{2 \tau} \boldsymbol {\sigma}} \left(\left(\boldsymbol {i} + \frac {\eta}{2 \tau} \boldsymbol {\sigma}\right) ^ {- 1} \left\{\frac {\beta}{2 \sqrt {n}} \boldsymbol {\sigma} ^ {1 / 2} \mathsf {p} _ {\boldsymbol {\mu}} ^ {\perp} \boldsymbol {h} - \frac {\tilde {\eta}}{2} \widetilde {\boldsymbol {\mu}} \right\}; \frac {\lambda}{q t ^ {q - 1}}\right), \tag {b.26} \\ \end{array}
$$

where we used symmetry of the distribution of  $\pmb{h}$ .

putting all pieces together in(b.25) we get

$$
\begin{array}{l} \min  _ {\boldsymbol {\theta}} \left\| \boldsymbol {\theta} \right\| _ {\ell_ {2}} ^ {2} + \frac {2 \lambda}{q t ^ {q - 1}} \left\| \boldsymbol {\theta} \right\| _ {\ell_ {q}} ^ {q} + \frac {\eta}{2 \tau} \left\| \boldsymbol {\sigma} ^ {\frac {1}{2}} \boldsymbol {\theta} \right\| _ {\ell_ {2}} ^ {2} + \frac {\beta}{\sqrt {n}} \boldsymbol {h} ^ {t} \mathsf {p} _ {\boldsymbol {\mu}} ^ {\perp} \boldsymbol {\sigma} ^ {1 / 2} \boldsymbol {\theta} + \widetilde {\eta} \widetilde {\boldsymbol {\mu}} ^ {t} \boldsymbol {\theta} \tag {b.27} \\ = 2 e _ {q, \boldsymbol {i} + \frac {\eta}{2 \tau} \boldsymbol {\sigma}} \left(\left(\boldsymbol {i} + \frac {\eta}{2 \tau} \boldsymbol {\sigma}\right) ^ {- 1} \left\{\frac {\beta}{2 \sqrt {n}} \boldsymbol {\sigma} ^ {1 / 2} \mathsf {p} _ {\boldsymbol {\mu}} ^ {\perp} \boldsymbol {h} - \frac {\tilde {\eta}}{2} \widetilde {\boldsymbol {\mu}} \right\}; \frac {\lambda}{q t ^ {q - 1}}\right) - \frac {\beta^ {2} \tau}{2 \delta \eta} \left(1 + \frac {2 \tau}{\eta} s _ {\rho} \left(- \frac {2 \tau}{\eta}\right)\right) \\ - \frac {\tilde {\eta} ^ {2}}{4 \left(1 + \frac {\eta}{2 \tau} a ^ {2}\right)}. \\ \end{array}
$$

using (b.27) in (b.23), the ao problem reduces to

$$
\begin{array}{l} \min_{\gamma \geq 0,\theta}\max_{\beta ,\lambda ,\eta \geq 0,\tilde{\eta}}\min_{\tau \geq 0,t\geq 0}2e_{q,i + \frac{\eta}{2\tau}\sigma}\left(\left(i + \frac{\eta}{2\tau}\sigma\right)^{-1}\left\{\frac{\beta}{2\sqrt{n}}\sigma^{1 / 2}\mathsf{p}_{\mu}^{\perp}\boldsymbol {h} - \frac{\tilde{\eta}}{2}\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\right\} ;\frac{\lambda}{qt^{q - 1}}\right) \\ - \frac {\beta^ {2} \tau}{2 \delta \eta} \left(1 + \frac {2 \tau}{\eta} s _ {\rho} \left(- \frac {2 \tau}{\eta}\right)\right) \\ - \frac {\tilde {\eta} ^ {2}}{4 \left(1 + \frac {\eta}{2 \tau} a ^ {2}\right)} + \beta \sqrt {\mathbb {e} \left[ \left(\left(1 + \varepsilon \gamma - \theta \| \boldsymbol {\mu} \| _ {\ell_ {2}}\right) + \alpha g\right) _ {+} ^ {2} \right]} \\ + 2 \lambda \frac {q - 1}{q} t - 2 \lambda \gamma + \frac {\eta \tau}{2} - \eta \alpha - \tilde {\eta} \theta \tag {b.28} \\ \end{array}
$$

scalarization of the auxiliary optimization problem. we proceed by defining  $\lambda_0 \coloneqq \frac{\lambda}{\|\pmb{\mu}\|_{\ell_p}}$ ,  $\gamma_0 \coloneqq \gamma \| \pmb{\mu}\|_{\ell_p}$  and  $t_0 \coloneqq t\|\pmb{\mu}\|_{\ell_p}$ . under assumptions 2 and 6, the asymptotic auxiliary optimization (ao) problem becomes

$$
\begin{array}{l} \min  _ {\alpha , \gamma_ {0} \geq 0, \theta} \max  _ {\beta , \lambda_ {0}, \eta \geq 0, \tilde {\eta}} \min  _ {\tau \geq 0, t _ {0} \geq 0} \quad 2 \mathsf {f} \left(\beta , \tilde {\eta}; \frac {\eta}{2 \tau}, \frac {\lambda_ {0}}{q t _ {0} ^ {q - 1}}\right) - \frac {\beta^ {2} \tau}{2 \delta \eta} \left(1 + \frac {2 \tau}{\eta} s _ {\rho} \left(- \frac {2 \tau}{\eta}\right)\right) \\ + 2 \lambda_ {0} \frac {q - 1}{q} t _ {0} - 2 \lambda_ {0} \gamma_ {0} + \frac {\eta \tau}{2} - \eta \alpha - \tilde {\eta} \theta \\ - \frac {\tilde {\eta} ^ {2}}{4 \left(1 + \frac {\eta}{2 \tau} a ^ {2}\right)} + \beta \sqrt {\mathbb {e} \left[ \left(\left(1 + \varepsilon_ {0} \gamma_ {0} - \theta v\right) + \alpha g\right) _ {+} ^ {2} \right]} \tag {b.29} \\ \end{array}
$$

here we used the relation  $\varepsilon = \varepsilon_0\| \pmb {\mu}\|_{\ell_p}$

we next solve for some of the variables in the ao problem by writing the kkt conditions.

1. define

$$
f \left(\frac {\eta}{\tau}\right) := 2 \mathsf {f} \left(\beta , \tilde {\eta}; \frac {\eta}{2 \tau}, \frac {\lambda_ {0}}{q t _ {0} ^ {q - 1}}\right) - \frac {\beta^ {2} \tau}{2 \delta \eta} \left(1 + \frac {2 \tau}{\eta} s _ {\rho} \left(- \frac {2 \tau}{\eta}\right)\right) - \frac {\tilde {\eta} ^ {2}}{4 (1 + \frac {\eta}{2 \tau} a ^ {2})},
$$

where we only made the dependence on  $\frac{\eta}{\tau}$  explicit in the notation  $f\left(\frac{\eta}{\tau}\right)$ . setting derivative with respect to  $\eta$  to zero, we obtain

$$
\frac {1}{\tau} f ^ {\prime} \left(\frac {\eta}{\tau}\right) + \frac {\tau}{2} - \alpha = 0. \tag {b.30}
$$

setting derivative with respect to  $\tau$  to zero, we obtain

$$
- \frac {\eta}{\tau^ {2}} f ^ {\prime} \left(\frac {\eta}{\tau}\right) + \frac {\eta}{2} = 0. \tag {b.31}
$$

combining (b.30) and (b.31), we get  $\eta(1 - \frac{\alpha}{\tau}) = 0$ . so either  $\alpha = \tau$  or  $\eta = 0$ . if  $\eta = 0$ , then it is clear that the terms involving  $\tau$  in the ao problem would vanish and therefore the value of  $\tau$  does not matter. so in this case, we can as well assume  $\tau = \alpha$ . this simplifies the ao problem by replacing for  $\tau$ :

$$
\begin{array}{l} \min  _ {\alpha , \gamma_ {0} \geq 0, \theta} \max  _ {\beta , \lambda_ {0}, \eta \geq 0, \tilde {\eta}} \min  _ {t _ {0} \geq 0} \quad 2 \mathsf {f} \left(\beta , \tilde {\eta}; \frac {\eta}{2 \alpha}, \frac {\lambda_ {0}}{q t _ {0} ^ {q - 1}}\right) - \frac {\beta^ {2} \alpha}{2 \delta \eta} \left(1 + \frac {2 \alpha}{\eta} s _ {\rho} \left(- \frac {2 \alpha}{\eta}\right)\right) \\ + 2 \lambda_ {0} \frac {q - 1}{q} t _ {0} - 2 \lambda_ {0} \gamma_ {0} - \frac {\eta \alpha}{2} - \tilde {\eta} \theta \\ - \frac {\tilde {\eta} ^ {2}}{4 \left(1 + \frac {\eta}{2 \alpha} a ^ {2}\right)} + \beta \sqrt {\mathbb {e} \left[ \left(\left(1 + \varepsilon_ {0} \gamma_ {0} - \theta v\right) + \alpha g\right) _ {+} ^ {2} \right]}. \tag {b.32} \\ \end{array}
$$

2. setting derivative with respect to  $\lambda_0$  to zero, we get

$$
2 \mathsf {f} _ {4} ^ {\prime} \left(\beta , \tilde {\eta}; \frac {\eta}{2 \alpha}, \frac {\lambda_ {0}}{q t _ {0} ^ {q - 1}}\right) \frac {1}{q t _ {0} ^ {q - 1}} + 2 \frac {q - 1}{q} t _ {0} - 2 \gamma_ {0} = 0, \tag {b.33}
$$

where  $\mathsf{f}_4^{\prime}$  denotes the derivative of function  $\mathsf{f}$  with respect to its forth argument. also, by setting derivative with respect to  $t_0$  to zero we get

$$
2 \mathrm {f} _ {4} ^ {\prime} \left(\beta , \tilde {\eta}; \frac {\eta}{2 \alpha}, \frac {\lambda_ {0}}{q t _ {0} ^ {q - 1}}\right) \lambda_ {0} \frac {1 - q}{q} t _ {0} ^ {- q} + 2 \lambda_ {0} \frac {q - 1}{q} = 0. \tag {b.34}
$$

combining (b.33) and (b.34) implies that

$$
2 \lambda_ {0} (q - 1) \left(\frac {\gamma_ {0}}{t _ {0}} - 1\right) = 0. \tag {b.35}
$$

therefore either  $\gamma_0 = t_0$  or  $\lambda_0 = 0$  or  $q = 1$ . if  $\lambda = 0$  or  $q = 1$  then the terms involving  $t_0$  in (b.32) vanish and hence we can assume  $t_0 = \gamma_0$  in this case as well. replacing  $t_0$  with  $\gamma_0$  in (b.32) we obtain

$$
\begin{array}{l} \min _ {\alpha , \gamma_ {0} \geq 0, \theta} \max _ {\beta , \lambda_ {0}, \eta \geq 0, \tilde {\eta}} \quad 2 \mathsf {f} \left(\beta , \tilde {\eta}; \frac {\eta}{2 \alpha}, \frac {\lambda_ {0}}{q \gamma_ {0} ^ {q - 1}}\right) - \frac {\beta^ {2} \alpha}{2 \delta \eta} \left(1 + \frac {2 \alpha}{\eta} s _ {\rho} \left(- \frac {2 \alpha}{\eta}\right)\right) - \frac {2 \lambda_ {0}}{q} \gamma_ {0} - \frac {\eta \alpha}{2} - \tilde {\eta} \theta \\ - \frac {\tilde {\eta} ^ {2}}{4 \left(1 + \frac {\eta}{2 \alpha} a ^ {2}\right)} + \beta \sqrt {\mathbb {e} \left[ \left(\left(1 + \varepsilon_ {0} \gamma_ {0} - \theta v\right) + \alpha g\right) _ {+} ^ {2} \right]}, \tag {b.36} \\ \end{array}
$$

which is the expression for  $d_{\mathrm{s}}(\alpha ,\gamma_0,\theta ,\beta ,\lambda_0,\eta ,\tilde{\eta})$  given by (6.2).

uniqueness and boundedness of the solution to ao problem. note that since  $\delta \leq \delta_{*}$ , by using theorem 6.1, we are in the separable regime and therefore optimization (f.2) is feasible with high probability and admits a bounded solution. this implies that the po problem (b.17) has bounded solution and since ao and po problems are asymptotically equivalent this implies that the ao problem (b.36) has bounded solution.

to show the uniqueness of the solution of (b.36), note that as we argued throughout the proof, its objective function  $d_{\mathrm{s}}$  is jointly strictly convex in  $(\alpha, \gamma_0, \theta)$  and jointly concave in  $(\beta, \lambda_0, \eta, \tilde{\eta})$ . therefore,  $\max_{\beta, \lambda_0, \eta, \tilde{\eta}} d_{\mathrm{s}}(\alpha, \gamma_0, \theta, \beta, \lambda_0, \eta, \tilde{\eta})$  is strictly convex in  $(\alpha, \gamma_0, \theta)$ . this follows from the fact that if a function  $f(\boldsymbol{x}, \boldsymbol{y})$  is strictly convex in  $\boldsymbol{x}$ , then  $\max_{\boldsymbol{y}} f(\boldsymbol{x}, \boldsymbol{y})$  is also strictly convex in  $\boldsymbol{x}$  and therefore has a unique minimizer  $(\alpha_*, \gamma_{0*}, \theta_*)$ .

part (b) of the theorem follows readily from our definition of parameters  $\alpha$ ,  $\theta$  and  $\gamma$ .

part (c) also follows from combining lemma 2.1 with part (b) of the theorem.

# b.3 proof of theorem 6.4

the goal of this theorem is to derive precise asymptotic behavior for the adversarially trained model  $\widehat{\pmb{\theta}}^{\varepsilon}$  given by

$$
\widehat {\boldsymbol {\theta}} ^ {\varepsilon} = \arg \min  _ {\boldsymbol {\theta} \in \mathbb {r} ^ {d}} \frac {1}{n} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \ell \left(y _ {i} \left\langle \boldsymbol {x} _ {i}, \boldsymbol {\theta} \right\rangle - \varepsilon \| \boldsymbol {\theta} \| _ {\ell_ {q}}\right). \tag {b.37}
$$

letting  $v_{i} \coloneqq y_{i}\langle \pmb{x}_{i},\pmb{\theta}\rangle$ , this optimization can be equivalently written as

$$
\min _ {\boldsymbol {\theta}, \boldsymbol {v} \in \mathbb {r} ^ {n}} \frac {1}{2 p} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \ell \left(v _ {i} - \varepsilon \left\| \boldsymbol {\theta} \right\| _ {\ell_ {q}}\right) \quad \mathrm {s u b j e c t t o} \boldsymbol {v} = \boldsymbol {d _ {y}} \boldsymbol {x} \boldsymbol {\theta},
$$

with  $d_{\mathbf{y}} = \mathrm{diag}(y_1, \ldots, y_n)$ . therefore, by writing the lagrangian by  $\mathbf{u} / n$  as the dual variable for the equality constraint, we arrive at

$$
\min _ {\pmb {\theta}, \pmb {v} \in \mathbb {r} ^ {n}} \max _ {\pmb {u} \in \mathbb {r} ^ {n}} \frac {1}{n} \bigl \{\pmb {u} ^ {\top} \pmb {d _ {y}} \pmb {x} \pmb {\theta} - \pmb {u} ^ {\top} \pmb {v} \bigr \} + \frac {1}{n} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \ell \left(v _ {i} - \varepsilon \left\| \pmb {\theta} \right\| _ {\ell_ {q}}\right)
$$

we next substitute for  $\pmb{x} = \pmb{y}\pmb{\mu}^{t} + \pmb{z}\pmb{\sigma}^{1 / 2}$ , under the gaussian mixtures model, which gives us

$$
\min  _ {\boldsymbol {\theta}, \boldsymbol {v} \in \mathbb {r} ^ {n}} \max  _ {\boldsymbol {u} \in \mathbb {r} ^ {n}} \frac {1}{n} \left\{\boldsymbol {u} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {1} \boldsymbol {\mu} ^ {t} \boldsymbol {\theta} + \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {d} _ {\boldsymbol {y}} \boldsymbol {z} \boldsymbol {\sigma} ^ {1 / 2} \boldsymbol {\theta} - \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {v} \right\} + \frac {1}{n} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \ell \left(v _ {i} - \varepsilon \| \boldsymbol {\theta} \| _ {\ell_ {q}}\right) \tag {b.38}
$$

note that by the above lagrangian is in a minimax problem in the form of  $\min_{\pmb{\theta}} \max_{\pmb{u}} \pmb{u}^t z \pmb{\theta} + \psi(\pmb{\theta}, \pmb{u})$ , with  $\pmb{z}$  standard gaussian matrix and  $\psi(\pmb{\theta}, \pmb{u})$  is convex in the minimization variable  $\pmb{\theta}$  and concave in the maximization variable  $\pmb{u}$ . this form allows us to apply the cgmt framework as outlined in section 7 and similar to the proof of theorem 6.3. but in order to do that, we need the minimization/maximization to be over compact sets. similar to the proof of theorem 6.3 we cope with this technical issue by introducing artificial boundedness constraints on the optimization variables that they do not change the optimal solution. specifically, we can add constraints of the form  $s_{\pmb{\theta}} = \{\pmb{\theta}: \| \pmb{\theta} \|_{\ell_q} \leq k_{\pmb{\theta}}\}$  and  $s_{\pmb{u}} = \{\pmb{u}: \| \pmb{u} \|_{\infty} \leq k_{\pmb{u}}\}$  for sufficiently large constants  $k_{\pmb{\theta}}, k_{\pmb{u}}$  without changing the optimal solution of (b.38). we refer to appendix e.3.1 for precise statements and proofs. this allows us to replace (b.38) with

$$
\min  _ {\boldsymbol {\theta} \in \mathcal {s} _ {\boldsymbol {\theta}}, \boldsymbol {v} \in \mathbb {r} ^ {n}} \max  _ {\boldsymbol {u} \in \mathcal {s} _ {\boldsymbol {u}}} \frac {1}{n} \left\{\boldsymbol {u} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {1} \boldsymbol {\mu} ^ {t} \boldsymbol {\theta} + \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\top} d _ {\boldsymbol {y}} z \sigma^ {1 / 2} \boldsymbol {\theta} - \boldsymbol {u} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {v} \right\} + \frac {1}{n} \sum_ {i = 1} ^ {n} \ell \left(v _ {i} - \varepsilon \| \boldsymbol {\theta} \| _ {\ell_ {q}}\right). \tag {b.39}
$$

# b.3.1 reduction to an auxiliary optimization problem via cgmt

next we define the projection matrices

$$
\mathsf {p} _ {\mu} ^ {\perp} := \boldsymbol {i} - \widetilde {\mu} \widetilde {\mu} ^ {t}, \quad \mathsf {p} _ {\mu} := \widetilde {\mu} \widetilde {\mu} ^ {t}
$$

and rewrite  $z\sigma^{1/2} = z\left(\mathsf{p}_{\mu} + \mathsf{p}_{\mu}^{\perp}\right)\sigma^{1/2}$ . since  $z\mathsf{p}_{\mu}$  and  $z\mathsf{p}_{\mu}^{\perp}$  are independent from each other the latter has the same distribution as

$$
\boldsymbol {z} \boldsymbol {\sigma} ^ {1 / 2} \sim \boldsymbol {z} \left(\boldsymbol {\sigma} ^ {1 / 2} \widetilde {\boldsymbol {\mu}}\right) ^ {t} + \boldsymbol {z} \mathsf {p} _ {\boldsymbol {\mu}} ^ {\perp} \boldsymbol {\sigma} ^ {1 / 2}. \tag {b.40}
$$

where  $\mathbf{z} \sim \mathcal{n}(0, i_n)$  and is independent from the matrix  $\mathbf{z}$ . thus the above optimization problem is equivalent to

$$
